## Page 1


´Etale Fundamental Groups
A geometric and topological approach to fundamental groups
in algebraic geometry
Loris De Vos
A thesis presented for the degree of
Master in Mathematics 2025-2026
Facult´e des Sciences
Supervisor: Prof. Dr. Joost Vercruysse
arXiv:2606.23906v1  [math.AG]  22 Jun 2026


## Page 2


Abstract
This thesis explores the notion of fundamental groups across three mathematical settings. We
begin with the classical topological theory of covering spaces, highlighting its structural analogy
with Galois theory. We then follow Grothendieck in transporting these ideas to algebraic geom-
etry. The inadequacy of the Zariski topology motivates the ´etale topology, from which the ´etale
fundamental group is constructed and compared to its topological counterpart via transcenden-
tal methods. Finally, we linearise the theory through Tannakian duality, where fundamental
groups are recovered as automorphism groups of fibre functors on certain monoidal categories,
a framework broad enough to encompass ´etale, topological, and motivic Galois groups alike.

## Page 5


Introduction
The aim of this thesis is threefold. It is first to give an overview of Grothendieck’s interpretation of
topological covering spaces and the fundamental group of a topological space. Then, to show how
it led to the realisation of similar concepts within the category of schemes. And finally, to link this
approach to the classical results of Tannaka and Krein.
The fundamental group of a topological space is an object which occupies a central place in
mathematics and, more precisely, in algebraic topology because it provides one of the first ways of
translating geometric phenomena into algebraic language. In the first chapter of this dissertation,
we will study the fundamental group through the scope of the theory of covering spaces. This ap-
proach is well-suited for the developments appearing later in the thesis because it reveals a deep
analogy between topology and Galois theory. Indeed, covering spaces behave in many respects like
field extensions: automorphism groups of coverings resemble Galois groups, universal covers play the
role of the algebraic closures, and the classification of coverings mirrors the classical correspondence
between intermediate field extensions and subgroups of its Galois group. We will then discuss a
generalisation by Krull of classical Galois theory for infinite field extensions, which takes advantage
of the topology endowed on profinite groups. This perspective emphasises the fact that the essential
structure underlying classical Galois theory is not tied specifically to fields, but rather to discrete
sets equipped with an action of a Galois group. From there, we will explain how covering spaces
fit into this broader framework by showing a similar reformulation for the classification of covering
spaces. The first chapter will end with a triple interpretation of covering spaces, as locally constant
sheaves and as representations of the fundamental group. This chapter will serve as both a review
of classical material and a conceptual foundation for the remainder of the thesis.
One of Grothendieck’s deepest insights was that an analogy similar to the one between Galois
theory and covering spaces could be transported into algebraic geometry. But the difficulty in doing
so lies in the fact that schemes do not possess a sufficiently rich topology. Indeed, for the Zariski
topology, many schemes become topologically simply connected, making the resulting fundamental
group trivial. Consequently, a new notion of topology had to be introduced. It led to the ´etale topol-
ogy, for which coverings are no longer given by open subsets, but by families of ´etale morphisms.
One possible axiomatic approach we could have taken in this work was to study the Galois category
of finite ´etale morphisms over connected schemes. The ´etale fundamental group would then appear
as the automorphism group of a certain fibre functor. This is done in [Gro03] and in [Len85]. In this
second chapter, we chose to follow a more direct approach, also presented in [Sza09], where the goal
is to construct the algebraic counterpart of the theory developed for topological spaces. This will
allow us to define an object analogous to the fundamental group of topological spaces called the ´etale
fundamental group. This can only be done after a thorough investigation of ´etale morphisms. Here
the reader will contemplate how our approach of the fundamental group through covering spaces
from the first chapter bears its fruits. The techniques we use to describe the ´etale fundamental
group are almost equivalent to the ones used to describe its topological counterpart. For the sake
of accuracy, we then present an approach that allows us to compare our two analogue fundamental
groups. It uses techniques called transcendental methods. These show that the ´etale fundamental
group of a connected scheme of finite type over C, is the profinite completion of the topological
fundamental group of the analytification of that scheme, provided we fix compatible base points.
We finish by giving a few structure theorems for the ´etale fundamental group, further deepening the
bridge between algebraic geometry, topology and arithmetic.
We end our investigation by a linearisation of the above concepts, replacing coverings by linear
algebraic data. This idea originates from the work of Tadao Tannaka and Marc Krein, and goes as
follows; rather than studying a (locally) compact group directly, one studies the monoidal (tensor)
category formed by its representations and attempts to recover the group from this category and
an associated fibre functor alone. Later, Grothendieck and Saavedra Rivano extended the theory to
affine group schemes. The third chapter aims to introduce the basic machinery of Tannakian cate-
gories and explain how we can define a Tannakian fundamental group scheme via the automorphism
group of the fibre functor. One of the remarkable aspects of this theory is its unifying character,
4


## Page 6


´etale fundamental groups, topological fundamental groups (and actually differential Galois groups
and motivic Galois groups) all arise naturally within the Tannakian framework. This highlights the
motivic character of the Tannakian fundamental group.
Notations and Conventions
Most of the notations we use in this thesis are defined in the Appendices, for example every
schemes are assumed to be separated.
Categories are usually represented by calligraphic capital letters such as C or bold abbreviations
such as Set, Rng, Sch, . . .
When considering group actions, we always assume that they act on a set from the left.
All rings are considered to be commutative.
We denote the algebraic closure of a field k by k, this is not to be confused with the geometric
point s when s P S for S a scheme.
And in the context of algebraic geometry all schemes are assumed to be Noetherian.
When A is a local ring, we denote its (unique) maximal ideal with a fraktur symbol, such as
p or m.
When f : X Ñ Y is a morphism of schemes, f # : A Ñ B is the induced homomorphism of
rings for the affine morphism f : U Ñ V with Spec B “ U Ă X and Spec A “ V Ă Y such
that fpUq Ă V .
Other notations are defined in the Appendices.

## Page 7


1
Topological Covering Spaces and Galois Theory
The fundamental group is a core object from algebraic topology; it is an algebraic invariant for
topological spaces. It is usually defined via homotopy of closed paths, and many powerful tools
can be used to compute them, such as the Seifert-Van Kampen theorem or deformation retracts.
However, in this thesis we will focus on a method which lies within the theory of covering spaces. In
this chapter, we describe how fundamental groups arise as automorphism groups of certain covering
spaces and present theorems classifying these covering spaces. Interestingly enough, the theory of
covering spaces is in some sense parallel to the theory of separable field extension; it has a certain
“Galois flavour” to it.
1.1
The Fundamental Group in Topology
We start by defining what homotopies and homotopy groups are. Let X be a topological space,
two paths on X are said to be homotopic if it is possible to continuously deform one into another.
Formally:
Definition 1.1.1. A homotopy of paths in X starting at x0 and ending at x1 is a family of paths
γt : r0, 1s Ñ X indexed by I such that
aq the endpoints are fixed : γtp0q “ x0, γtp1q “ x1 , and
bq the map H : I ˆ r0, 1s Ñ X ; pt, sq ÞÑ γtpsq is continuous.
Two paths f and g are said to be homotopic if there is a homotopy of paths between them and we
write f » g.
It is a straightforward verification to see that the relation “γ1 is homotopic to γ2” is an equivalence
relation. Let’s denote ΣpX, xq the set of loops in X based at x, now path-homotopy of loops defines
an equivalence relation on ΣpX, xq.
Definition 1.1.2. A continuous map f : X Ñ Y is called a homotopy equivalence if there exists
a continuous map g : Y Ñ X such that g ˝ f » idX and f ˝ g » idY . In this case, the spaces X and
Y are said to be homotopy equivalent.
Definition 1.1.3. The fundamental group of X based at x, denoted by π1pX, xq, is the set of
homotopic classes of loops based at x.
The fundamental group is also often called the first homotopy group. The subscript 1 in the funda-
mental group notation hints at the existence of higher homotopy groups πnpX, xq, which are similarly
considered as sets of homotopy classes based on maps f : pSn, sq Ñ pX, xq. These higher homotopy
groups “detect” higher-dimensional “holes” and the situation becomes much more complicated. In
this thesis, we focus on π1pX, xq, which already gives us a very rich theory.
The set of loops equipped with the homotopy equivalence relation is indeed a group with its operation
being the concatenation of paths denoted by ‚ and its inverses correspond to reversing the direction
of paths. Given two paths γ1, γ2 : r0, 1s Ñ X with γ1p1q “ γ2p0q we define their concatenation
γ1 ‚ γ2 : r0, 1s Ñ X by

We say that a topological space is simply-connected if and only if its fundamental group is trivial.
Remark 1. Let f : pX, xq Ñ pY, yq be a continuous map between pointed topological spaces such
that fpxq “ y, then we denote by f˚ : π1pX, xq Ñ π1pY, yq the induced homomorphism, obtained by
composition, f˚prγsq :“ rf ˝ γs for any rγs P π1pX, xq.
As expected, the fundamental group is a topological invariant :

## Page 8


Proposition 1.1.4. Homeomorphic spaces have isomorphic fundamental groups. If f : X Ñ Y is
a homeomorphism, then f˚ : π1pX, xq Ñ π1pY, fpxqq is an isomorphism.
Proof. If f is a homeomorphism, then pf ´1q˚ ˝ f˚ “ pf ´1 ˝ fq˚ “ pidXq˚ “ idπ1pX,xq. Similarly,
f˚ ˝ pf ´1q˚ is the identity on π1pY, fpxqq.
Similarly, one proves the following.
Corollary 1.1.5. Two homotopic equivalent spaces have the same fundamental group. If f : X Ñ Y
is a homotopy equivalence, then f˚ : π1pX, xq Ñ π1pY, fpxqq is an isomorphism.
One thing to notice is that if X is not path-connected, the fundamental groups based at points
in different path-connected components don’t have any relation to each other.
Therefore, it is
customary to consider path-connected topological spaces when studying fundamental groups. The
next proposition tells us that in a path-connected space, the fundamental group is insensitive to
changes of base points. Thus, when this is clear, we will sometimes omit to write the base point.
Proposition 1.1.6. If X is a path-connected topological space, then π1pX, x0q and π1pX, x1q are
isomorphic for any x0, x1 P X.
Proof. Since two points x0 and x1 may be joined by a path γ starting at x0 and ending at x1,
π1pX, x0q is non-canonically isomorphic to π1pX, x1q via δ ÞÑ γ ‚ δ ‚ γ´1.
Example 1.1.7. Of course, since Rn is contractible, any loop is homotopic to the constant loop, so
π1pRn, xq “ t1u for any base point x P Rn.
Computing non-trivial fundamental groups such as π1pS1q is already harder. An alternative is to
use the theory of covering spaces and lifting of paths. The calculation of π1pS1q however, has many
applications. It allows one to give a proof of the Fundamental Theorem of Algebra, to show the
Brouwer’s fixed-point theorem in dimension 2 or even to prove the Borsuk-Ulam theorem in dimen-
sion 2 (these theorems with their proofs can be found in [Hat02] as Theorem 1.8, 1.9 and 1.10
respectively).
In the language of categories, the fundamental group is a covariant functor which assigns to each
pointed topological space pX, xq its fundamental group based at x, and to each pointed continuous
map f : pX, xq Ñ pX1, x1q with fpxq “ x1 its induced homomorphism f˚ : π1pX, xq Ñ π1pX1, fpxqq.
1.2
Covering Spaces and their Galois structure
In this section, we introduce the theory of covering spaces. Then we show that it exhibits a structure
that behaves similarly to that of field extensions. Later on, we will prove the existence of a so-called
universal covering that is deeply related to the fundamental group.
Definition 1.2.1. A covering of a topological space X consists of a topological space Y and a
map p : Y Ñ X with the following property : for any x P X there exists a neighbourhood U of x
such that

covers p : Y Ñ X and q : Z Ñ X to be a continuous map f : Y Ñ Z making the following diagram
commute,

This makes a category over the topological space X, whose objects are covers of X and morphisms
are morphisms of covers of X, called the category of covers of X.

Remark 2. In the following, we will assume that X is (path)-connected, otherwise we can simply
consider coverings of connected components individually. The definition yields that each fibre p´1pxq
is a discrete set for any x P X, since each sheet Vy contains a unique point of p´1pxq, namely y. If
this set is finite for any x P X, then the number of sheets #p´1pxq is constant over U and (since X
is connected) constant over X.
For the purposes of our thesis, we further assume that all our topological spaces are locally path-
connected and semi-locally simply-connected. These assumption can be understood as the technical
bedrock for covering space theory to hold.
However, as our first example shows, we do not require that the above covering space must be
connected :
Examples 1.2.2.
aq Let I be a non-empty discrete topological space and form the topological product X ˆ I. The
projection on the first component p : X ˆ I Ñ X turns X ˆ I into a cover of X with fibre I.
We call this the trivial cover.
bq Consider the circle S1, with a map p : R Ñ S1 defined by pptq “ e2πit. This map is clearly a
connected covering of S1, locally S1 is homeomorphic to R, and it is even an infinitely sheeted
covering. An insightful picture is to think about the covering as an infinite helix above the
circle.
In this sense, every covering space can be characterised as being locally trivial. And if X is con-
nected, the fibres of p are all homeomorphic to the same discrete space I.
Of course the composition of two coverings maps is still a covering map where the number of sheets
might be greater. We also have the following proposition about composition of coverings. We say
that a cover is connected is the covering space is connected.
Proposition 1.2.3. Let q : Z Ñ X be a connected cover and f : Y Ñ Z be a continuous map. If
the composition q ˝ f : Y Ñ X is a cover, then so is f : Y Ñ X.
Proof. We first show that for any open neighbourhood V of z P Z, its preimage by f is a disjoint
union of open sets such that each of them map homeomorphically onto V .
Let x “ qpzq and U be an open neighbourhood of x such that its preimages by q and p “ q ˝ f are

fpWjq Ă Vi. Which is actually a homeomorphism since both fpWjq and Vi are homeomorphically
mapped onto U by q. This shows that the preimage of any Vi by f is a disjoint union of Wj’s.
Now we show that f is surjective. First notice that the argument above implies that fpY q is open in
Z. Since Z is connected it is enough to now prove that ZzfpY q is also open. If z P ZzfpY q and U
is a neighbourhood of x “ qpzq as above, then the whole component Vi of q´1pxq containing z must
be disjoint from fpY q, otherwise the whole component would have to be contained in fpY q which is
a contradiction. This shows that f is surjective, concluding the proof.
Powerful tools in the study of covering spaces are the following lifting lemmas:
Lemma 1.2.4 (Lifting (Homotopy) Property). Let p : Y Ñ X be a cover, y a point of Y and
x “ ppyq.
• Given a path γ : r0, 1s Ñ X starting at x, there is a unique lift rγ : r0, 1s Ñ Y with rγp0q “ y
and p ˝ rγ “ γ.
• For each homotopy h : I ˆ r0, 1s Ñ X such that hpt, 0q “ x for all t P I, there is a unique lift
rh : I ˆ r0, 1s Ñ Y such that rhpt, 0q “ y for all t P I.
Proof. See [Har83] (Prop.1.30 and 1.34).
Lemma 1.2.5 (Lifting Criterion). Let p : Y Ñ X be a connected covering and f : Z Ñ X be a
continuous map. Then a lift rf : Z Ñ Y of f exists if and only if f˚pπ1pZ, zqq is a subgroup of
p˚pπ1pY, yqq for py, zq P Y ˆ Z.

As the above lifting properties suggest, there is an intimate connection between covering maps and
fundamental groups. The key to further understanding this connection is a natural action on each
fibre of a covering by the fundamental group of the base called the monodromy action.
Theorem 1.2.7 (The Monodromy Action). Let p : Y Ñ X be a connected covering and x P X.
There is a transitive left action of π1pX, xq on the fibre p´1pxq called the monodromy action, given
by rγs ¨ y “ rγyp1q for y P p´1pxq, rγs P π1pX, xq and rγy the unique lift of γ in Y starting at y.
Proof. Let any y P p´1pxq, then the path-lifting property 1.2.4 associates to every loop γ based at
x in X a unique lift which is a path rγy starting at y and finishing at rγyp1q P p´1pxq. By lifting of
homotopies we have that if γ1 „ γ2, then rγ1y „ rγ2y, so rγyp1q depends only on the path-class of γ
(rγs ¨ y is now well defined). Let us prove that this is indeed a group action. Since the constant path
is the unique lift of the constant loop rcs ¨ y “ rcyp1q “ y. Suppose γ and δ are two loops based at
x and let z “ rγs ¨ y “ rγyp1q, then rδs ¨ prγs ¨ yq “ rδzp1q. And rδz ‚ rγy being the lift of δ ‚ γ at y, we

And finally since Y is path-connected, any two points y, y1 in p´1pxq are joined by a path ω in Y .
Setting γ “ p ˝ ω (ω becomes a loop when its endpoints are collapsed together by p), we see that
ω is actually a lift of γ and therefore by monodromy action rγs ¨ y “ y1. The action is transitive on
p´1pxq.
Below we prove a theorem that is a direct consequences of the lifting properties.
Theorem 1.2.8. Let p : Y Ñ X and q : Z Ñ X be two coverings.
aq If two morphism of coverings from p to q agree at one point of Y , then they are equal.
bq Fixing x P X, any morphism between the coverings p and q restrict to a π1pX, xq-equivariant
map from p´1pxq to q´1pxq (with respect to the monodromy action).
Proof. A morphism φ : Y Ñ Z of covers can also be seen as a lift of p : Y Ñ X. Thus uniqueness
follows from uniqueness of the lifting property proving aq.
Suppose φ : Y Ñ Z is a morphism of covers (from p to q). By definition, φ maps p´1pxq to q´1pxq.
Now, given x P X, y P p´1pxq and rγs P π1pX, xq, we need to show that φprγs ¨ yq “ rγs ¨ φpyq. Let
rγy be the lift of γ to a path in Y starting at y, and consider the path φ ˝ rγy in Z. Its initial point
is φ ˝ rγyp0q “ φpyq and it satisfies q ˝ φ ˝ rγy “ p ˝ rγy, so φ ˝ rγy is the lift of γ to Z starting at φpyq.
Therefore, by the monodromy action,
rγs ¨ φpyq “ pφ ˝ rγyqp1q “ φprγyp1qq “ φprγs ¨ yq.
A clever way of retrieving new covering spaces from existing ones, is to consider their quotient by
some group action. Let us define the appropriate group action needed in this case :
Definition 1.2.9. A group G acting on a topological space X by homeomorphisms (here we may
understand by automorphisms) is said to act properly discontinuously if for each x P X, there
exist an open neighbourhood U of x such that pg ¨ Uq X U “ H for all g P G and g ‰ 1. Properly
discontinuous actions are also called covering space actions.
9


## Page 11


Proposition 1.2.10. Let Y be a connected topological space and let G act on Y properly discontin-
uously. Then the quotient map pG : Y Ñ Y {G is a covering.
Proof. The map pG is surjective and moreover each x P Y {G has an open neighbourhood of the
form U “ pGpV q with V such that V X pg ¨ V q “ H for all g P G with g ‰ 1. And U satisfies the
conditions of Definition 1.2.1 so that Y becomes a covering of Y {G.
We now introduce a particular group which has the property of acting properly discontinuously on
covering spaces.
Definition 1.2.11. Let p : Y Ñ X be a covering. A deck transformation is a homeomorphism
d : Y Ñ Y such that the following diagram commutes.

Together with composition, the set of deck transformations forms a group denoted Deckppq or
AutpY |Xq pthe second notation may be familiar to the reader, as it resembles the notation used
to describe Galois groupsq.
Notice that for any x P X, the automorphism group AutpY |Xq sends all of p´1pxq to itself. We
further assume that AutpY |Xq acts on Y by the left.
Corollary 1.2.12. A homeomorphism d P AutpY |Xq of a connected covering p : Y Ñ X having a
fixed point must be trivial.
Proof. This is immediate from Theorem 1.2.8 if we take one (auto)morphism of covers to be the
deck transformation d and another one to be the identity.
Proposition 1.2.13. If p : Y Ñ X is a connected covering, the action of AutpY |Xq on Y is properly
discontinuous.
Proof. Let y P Y and set x “ ppyq. Let U be a connected open neighbourhood of x such that p´1pUq
is a disjoint union of open sets Vi as in the Definition 1.2.1, where one of these opens, let’s say Vi,
contains y. For d P AutpY |Xq non-trivial, d maps Vi isomorphically onto some Vj by definition of
a deck transformation. Since Y is connected, we can apply Corollary 1.2.12 and since d ‰ idY , we
must have i ‰ j. This satisfies the condition of properly discontinuity.
One more thing can be said about the group G, acting properly discontinuously on the connected
topological spaces Y and the associate quotient map pG : Y Ñ Y {G from Proposition 1.2.10:
Proposition 1.2.14. If G is a group acting properly discontinuously on a connected space Y , the
automorphism group of the covering pG : Y Ñ Y {G is precisely G.
Proof. Since G acts by homeomorphism on Y and p sends any y P Y to ppyq P Y {G, then ppyq “ ppg¨
yq by properly discontinuity of the action of G onto Y , therefore G is a subgroup of AutpY |pY {Gqq.
Now, let ϕ P AutpY, pY {Gqq, and y P Y . Since the fibres of pG are exactly the orbits of G (we
quotient Y by it), we may find g P G such that ϕpyq “ g ¨ y. Now applying Corollary 1.2.12 to the
homeomorphism ϕ ˝ g´1, we get g “ ϕ.
From the previous propositions, it is now obvious that for a connected covering p : Y Ñ X, we have
the following decomposition p “ p ˝ pAutpY |Xq : Y

Definition 1.2.15. We say that a covering p : Y Ñ X is Galois or Normal if Y is connected and
if the map p defined above is a homeomorphism.
The previous proposition already gives us an example of Galois coverings. These types of coverings
will turn out to be important in the classification of covering spaces (Theorem 1.2.19), playing a
similar role to Galois field extensions in the Main Theorem of Galois.

the identity and thus ϕ “ ϕ1, meaning that the action is not only transitive but also free.

stabiliser of y under the monodromy action is p˚pπ1pY, yqq ď π1pX, xq. And as y varies over the

The following lemma is actually a criterion for deciding whether the morphism of coverings of
Theorem 1.2.8 is actually an isomorphism.
Lemma 1.2.17. Let p : Y Ñ X and q : Z Ñ X be two connected coverings.
Y and Z are isomorphic if and only if, for some x P X the conjugacy classes of subgroups of π1pX, xq
induced by q1 and q2 are the same.
Proof. First assume that Y and Z are isomorphic. For any x P X, this isomorphism restrict to a
π1pX, xq-equivariant bijection from p´1pxq to q´1pxq as in Theorem 1.2.8. So the sets of stabilisers of
points in p´1pxq and in q´1pxq are in the same conjugacy class subgroup as Theorem A.2.1 suggests.
And since these groups are exactly the induced subgroups p˚pπ1pY, yqq and q˚pπ1pZ, zqq form Lemma
1.2.16, they must be conjugate.
Now suppose that p and q induce the same conjugacy class of subgroups for some x P X. Choosing
y P Y arbitrarily, by Lemma 1.2.16, there is some z P Z such that p˚pπ1pY, yqq “ q˚pπ1pZ, zqq. By
the Lifting Criterion, there exist homomorphism of coverings f : Z Ñ Y and g : Y Ñ Z (since
they are essentially lifts) with fpzq “ y and gpyq “ z. The composites f ˝ g and g ˝ f are covering
homomorphism fixing z and y respectively, so by Corollary 1.2.12, they are identities, there is an
isomorphism of coverings between p and q.
Finally we get to the following characterisation,
Theorem 1.2.18. Let p : Y Ñ X be a connected covering.
The following conditions are equivalent :
aq p is a Galois covering.
bq Y is connected and AutpY |Xq acts transitively on the fibre p´1pxq for any x P X.
cq The stabiliser p˚pπ1pY, yqq is a normal subgroup of π1pX, xq for any y P p´1pxq.
Proof. aq ô bq ; by definition, the underlying set of Y { AutpY |Xq is the AutpY |Xq-orbits of Y . So
the map p is one to one precisely when each such orbits is equal to a whole fibre of p or equivalently
that AutpY |Xq acts transitively on each fibre. And since X is connected, all fibres are homeomorphic
to the same discrete space.
bq ô cq ; fix x P X.
Combining Lemmas 1.2.16 and 1.2.17, we have that AutpY |Xq acts
transitively on p´1pxq if and only if the subgroups p˚pπ1pY, yqq are the same for all y P Y . And
since they are conjugacy classes, it is equivalent to saying that p˚pπ1pY, yqq is a normal subgroup of
π1pX, xq.

We now touch upon the classification of topological covering spaces, which is surprisingly similar
to the Galois correspondence of field extensions, here, Galois covers play the role of Galois field
extensions, the Galois groups are automorphism groups of covers, and as we will see later, universal
coverings are separable closures.
Theorem 1.2.19 (Main Classification of Coverings). Let X be a connected topological space, p :
Y Ñ X be a Galois cover and G the group automorphisms of p. If q : Z Ñ X is a connected cover
such that p “ q ˝ f for f : Y Ñ Z, then f is a Galois cover and Z – Y {H (where H “ AutpY |Zq
is a subgroup of G).
The maps H ÞÑ Y {H and Z ÞÑ AutpY |Zq induce a bijection between the
subgroups of G and the intermediate covers Z as above. The cover q : Z Ñ X is Galois if and only
if AutpY |Zq “ H is a normal subgroup of G (in that case, AutpZ|Xq “ G{H.
Proof. By hypothesis, the map p factors as the composite p ˝ pH with p “ Y {H Ñ X and pH :
Y Ñ Y {H for H a subgroup of G. Since p and pH are coverings (Proposition 1.2.10), then p is
continuous. Moreover, p is a covering, so by definition, there exists an open set U in X such that
p´1pUq – U ˆ I and the subgroup H of G acts on I. So we have that p´1pUq is open subset of Y {H
with p´1pUq “– U ˆ J where J is the set of H-orbits of I. Hence p is a cover since locally (for a
sufficiently small open set) it is a trivial cover.
Now suppose that q : Z Ñ X is a connected cover with p “ q ˝ f as above. Then by Proposition
1.2.3, f : Y Ñ Z is a covering map as well. To see that it is moreover a Galois cover, by the
characterisation 1.2.18 of Galois coverings, we need to check that AutpY |Zq acts transitively on a
fibres of f. Let z P Z and y1, y2 P f ´1pzq. Then y1, y2 P p´1pqpzqq because f ´1pzq Ă p´1pqpzqq.
Since p is a Galois covering, there exists ϕ P AutpY |Xq such that y1 “ ϕpy2q. We are done if we
show that actually ϕ P AutpY |Zq, which is equivalent to saying that the set ty P Y | fpyq “ fpϕpyqqu
is equal to the whole of Y . But this follows from Theorem 1.2.8, so we have that f “ f ˝ ϕ and
ty P Y | fpyq “ fpϕpyqqu “ Y and ϕ P AutpY |Zq. The above two constructions being inverse to
each other, only the last statement is left to prove.
One implication is easy. Since p is a Galois covering, if H is a normal subgroup of G, then G{H acts
transitively on fibres of q in Z “ Y {H Ă Y . Making q : Z Ñ X a Galois cover. And by definition

of Galois covers. Our group homomorphism h is well-defined since φ is unique, for if ψ P AutpZ|Xq
was another such automorphism, by Theorem 1.2.8, ψ – φ. By the same argument, the maps φ ˝ f

1.3
Universal Cover and Fundamental Group
In the previous section, we discussed the concept of subcovers, or covers that cover other covers. We
now introduce a new type of covering space, which can be thought of as the covering of all coverings,
inherently called the universal cover.
Definition 1.3.1. Let X be a path-connected, locally simply-connected topological space and pick a
base point x P X. The covering q : r
Xx Ñ X is called an universal cover if r
Xx is simply-connected.
It is important to note that universal covers do not exists in general.
But for sufficiently nice
topological spaces (path-connected, simply path-connected) they always exists, for more on this, we
refer to [Hat02] page 63.

Xx of X.
The points of r

Xx has a canonical
point rx corresponding to the constant path on x. This construction is motivated by the fact that
r
Xx has to be simply-connected, thus any two points of r
Xx can be joined by a unique homotopy
class of paths. The projection q : r
Xx Ñ X is defined as follows: take a point ry P r
Xx consider its
representing path γ : r0, 1s Ñ X starting at x and ending at y, and set qpryq “ γp1q “ y.
Simply-connected covers constructed that way are endowed with the following topology: consider as
a basis of open neighbourhoods of ry P r
Xx, the sets rU. To define rU, we start with a simply-connected

set of homotopy classes of paths obtained by composing the homotopy class of f with the homotopy

obtained by “continuing homotopy classes of paths arriving at y to other points of U”. Given two
neighbourhoods rUry and rVry of ry, there exists Ă
Wry for some simply-connected neighbourhood W of y,
contained in U X V .
Another consequence of the lifting criterion is that a universal cover r
X of X is unique up to isomor-
phism.

the category of coverings spaces CovpXq of X to the category π1pX, xq-Set of sets equipped with a
π1pX, xq-action, by sending a cover p : Y Ñ X to the fibre p´1pxq.

The following theorem describes the connection between universal covers and fundamental groups.
Recall that in group theory, for a group G, its opposite group, denoted Gop is the group with the
same elements as G but with group operation defined by px, yq ÞÑ y ¨x (of course, G – Gop). In that
case, left actions become right actions and vice versa.

We finish our introduction on covering spaces and topological fundamental groups by invoking a
result about compact manifolds.
Proposition 1.3.10. A compact manifold is homotopy equivalent to a CW complex.
Proof. See [Hat02], Corollary A.12.
And as consequence of the Van Kampen Theorem, we have the following application for CW com-
plexes:
Theorem 1.3.11. The fundamental group of a CW complex is finitely presented.
Proof. See [Hat02], Chapter 1.2, Applications to Cell Complexes.
From this, for every compact manifold M, its fundamental group π1pMq is finitely presented.

1.4
A Reformulation of Classical Galois Theory
In this section, we present a reformulation of the famous classification of field extensions by Galois-
correspondence. It was an idea originated by Grothendieck who recognised that the Galois corre-
spondence was really a statement about group action and sets, we shall refer to such realisations with
the French expression “`a la Grothendieck”. This reformulation could be seen as a genesis for this
thesis since this idea motivated Grothendieck to develop an analogous theory for the classification of
topological covering spaces. With these two reformulations at hand, Grothendieck, in his more than
influential S´eminaires de G´eom´etrie Alg´ebrique [Gro03] developed the theory of finite ´etale covering
for algebraic spaces, which sits above the intersection between field extensions and covering spaces.
Grothendieck’s idea was to study field extensions through the prism of category theory, by showing
the existence of a category equivalence between separable field extensions and sets on which the
absolute Galois group acts in a certain way. For that, we need a stronger result within the theory
of Galois, related to infinite field extensions and profinite groups. This theorem was proven by W.
Krull in the 1930’s, for that he introduced a topological structure on Galois groups.
In the following, the requisite background for classical Galois theory is assumed.
However, for
the sake of clarity, we recall the Main Theorem of Galois theory, which provides the definitive
classification of separable field extensions.
Theorem 1.4.1 (Main Theorem of Galois Theory). Let L|k be a finite Galois extension with Galois
group G. The maps H ÞÑ LH and M ÞÑ GalpL|Mq yield an inclusion-reversing bijection between
the set of subgroups of G and the set of intermediate fields between L and k pfor H a subgroup of G
and M an intermediate field between L and kq. Moreover the extension L|M is always Galois and
the extension M|k is Galois if ans only if H is a normal subgroup of G (GalpM|kq – G{H).
Proof. The material needed and the proof itself of the main theorem of Galois can be found in
[Lan02] (Chapter V I. 1) .
From classical Galois theory for field extensions, we obtain the following lemma (mostly from the
primitive element theorem, Theorem 4.6 in [Lan02])
Lemma 1.4.2. Let K|k be a Galois field extension (possibly infinite). Then each finite subextension
of K|k can be embedded in a finite Galois subextension of K|k called the Galois closure.
Proof. By the theorem of the primitive element, each finite subextension is simple (of the form kpαq)
and we may embed kpαq into the splitting field of a the minimal polynomial of α which is Galois
over k.
1.4.1
Profinite Groups
As the name suggests, the notion of a topological group is one of the most natural bridges between
group theory and topology. A topological group G is a topological space for which the underlying
set is also a group. We also require that the group operations and the inversion maps are continuous.
A homomorphism of topological groups is defined to be a continuous group homomorphism G Ñ H.
Together they form a category. Since topological groups are still groups, they can act on all kinds
of sets, below we give a necessary and sufficient condition to determine whether an action of a
topological group on a discrete topological space is continuous or not.
Definition 1.4.3. We say that an action of a topological group G on a topological space X is a
continuous group action if the map m : G ˆ X Ñ X; pg, xq ÞÑ g ¨ x is a continuous map.
Lemma 1.4.4. Let X be a discrete topological space, and G a topological group acting on X. We
say that G has a continuous action on X if and only if for each x P X, the stabiliser Gx is open.
Proof. Suppose that Gx is open. Since X has the discrete topology, every x is open and

An important class of topological groups are profinite groups. Profinite objects arise in category
theory as the inverse limit of an inverse system of finite objects.
Definition 1.4.5. A profinite group is a group that is isomorphic to the inverse limit of an inverse
system of discrete finite groups. An inverse system consists of a directed set pI, ďq, an indexed family
of finite groups tGi | i P Iu and a family of homomorphisms tϕij : Gj Ñ Gi | i, j P I, i ď ju such
that ϕii is the identity map and the collection satisfied ϕij ˝ ϕjk “ ϕik whenever i ď j ď k. The
inverse limit is then the set,

Remark 5. We can endow a profinite group with a natural topology as follows: let G be an inverse
limit of the system of finite groups pGα, ϕαβq and endow Gα with the discrete topology, their product
with the product topology and the subgroup G Ă ś Gα with the subspace topology.
With this in mind, we see that the projection homomorphisms pri : lim
ÐÝ Gi Ñ Gi are continuous,
and their kernels form a basis of open neighbourhoods of 1 in G (the last statement holds since for
every non-trivial element g, there exists some coordinate i such that pripgq ‰ 1 by definition of the
inverse limit).
And by translations, we can then obtain a basis of open normal subgroups of neighbourhoods of any
elements g P G (note that translations are homeomorphisms)
Another famous characterisation of profinite group is the following:
Theorem 1.4.6. Profinite group are equivalently groups for which the underlying set is compact,
totally disconnected and Hausdorff.
Proof. See [RZ10] (Theorem 2.1.3)
Corollary 1.4.7. Closed subgroups of profinite groups are again profinite.
Proof. Since closed subsets of a compact Hausdorff space are precisely the compact subsets and
subsets of totally disconnected Hausdorff spaces are again totally disconnected and Hausdorff. Closed
subgroups are again profinite by the above characterisation.
Corollary 1.4.8. A subgroup of a profinite groups is open if and only if it is closed and of finite
index.
Proof. Every open subgroup U in a topological group is closed.
Indeed, for topological groups,
translations are homeomorphisms, so U ÞÑ gU is a homeomorphism and since the complement of U
is given by the disjoint union of open cosets gU, it is also closed. By compactness, the number of
these cosets must be finite hence the subgroup is of finite index. Conversely, a closed subgroup of
finite index is open, being the complement of the finite disjoint union of its cosets, which are closed
by homeomorphism.
Lemma 1.4.9. Let G be a profinite group. Then the intersection of all open normal subgroups of
G is trivial.
Proof. Since G is Hausdorff, for any element g P G, there exist two open sets U and V in G with
1 P U and g P V such that U X V “ H. But since U contains 1, from the basis of normal open
neighbourhoods tUi | i P Iu (from Remark 5), there exists an open normal subgroup Ui Ă U
which contains 1. Thus Ui X V “ H, so g R Ş
iPI Ui. Hence the only element that lies in every
neighbourhood of 1 is 1 itself.

Lemma 1.4.10. Let G be a profinite group, and H Ă G a closed subgroup.

Proof. For the first statement, given g P GzH, we shall find an open subgroup of G containing H but
not g. Since H is closed, and G is Hausdorff, there exists an open neighbourhood U of g such that
U X H “ H. From Remark 5, by translation we can take an open subgroup N in H, normal in G
such that gN is a basis element of the neighbourhood system at g, this gives us gN X H “ H. Then
the open subgroup pr´1

For the second statement, we use that V 1 is closed in G and by the first statement, we write both V 1
and H as the intersection of the open subgroups of G containing them. But since rH : V 1s is finite
(by the above corollaries), we find finitely many open subgroups V1, ..., Vn in G containing V 1 but
not H such that V 1 “ V1 X ... X Vn X H. Thus V “ V1 X ... X Vn proves the last statement.
Sometimes, we want to endow a group with a profinite group structure (in the sense of Theorem 1.4.6)
even when it is not possible. For example, the group Z cannot be seen as a profinite group; we cannot
endow it with a topology that is simultaneously compact, totally disconnected and Hausdorff. When
this is the case, we can “force” a profinite group structure by considering the profinite completion
of a group.
Definition 1.4.11. Given an arbitrary group G, the profinite completion ˆG of G is defined as
the inverse limit of the groups G{N, where N runs through the normal subgroups in G of finite
index (these normal subgroups are partially ordered by inclusion, which makes them into an inverse
system of natural homomorphisms between the quotients).
Remark 6. While it is not always the case that the profinite completion of a finitely presented
group is finitely presented, it is the case that the profinite completion of a finitely generated group
is topologically finitely generated (see [RZ10], Section 2.12.2).
We say that a profinite group is
topologically finitely generated if it contains a dense finitely generated subgroup.
The next proposition realises Galois groups of Galois extensions as profinite groups
Proposition 1.4.12. Let K|k be a Galois field extension (possibly infinite). The Galois groups of
finite Galois subextensions of K|k together with the homomorphisms ϕML : GalpM|kq Ñ GalpL|kq
form an inverse system whose inverse limit is isomorphic to GalpK|kq.

Galois subextension containing M. We only have to prove the isomorphism part of the statement.

From the previous proposition and by the definition of profinite groups, we can indeed conclude that
Galois groups are profinite. And as long as one does not specify the ground field, the opposite is also
true. This was proven in two steps by C. Jordan : finite symmetric groups arise as Galois groups for
some field extension (see Example V I.2.4 in [Lan02]). And then in [Wat74], C. Waterhouse, showed
that profinite groups are always realisable as the Galois group of some (infinite) field extension.
18


## Page 20


The case for which the base field is fixed to be the rational numbers Q however, remains an open
problem, famously referred to as the Inverse Galois Problem.
Now we can prove the main theorem of Galois theory for infinite field extensions (this was our main
reason to introduce topological groups):
Theorem 1.4.13 (Krull). Let K|k be a Galois extension (possibly infinite) with Galois group G.
If L is a subextension of K|k, then GalpK|Lq is a closed group of G. The maps H ÞÑ KH and
L ÞÑ GalpK|Lq yield an inclusion-reversing bijection between the set of closed subgroups of G and
the set of intermediate fields between K and k (for H a closed subgroup of G and M an intermediate
field between K and k). And a subextension L|k is Galois if and only if GalpL|kq is normal in G (in
that case, GalpL|kq – G{H).
Proof. Let L|k be a finite separable extension in K|k. By Lemma 1.4.2, we can embed L|k in a finite
Galois extension M|k in K|k. Then GalpM|kq is a finite quotient of GalpK|kq “ G since the latter
is profinite and GalpM|Lq is a subgroup of GalpM|kq by the main theorem of Galois theory 1.4.1.
Now consider UL the inverse image of GalpM|Lq by the natural projection prM : G Ñ GalpM|kq.
UL is then open because GalpM|kq has the discrete topology.
Notice that UL “ GalpK|Lq. Indeed, each element of UL fixed L since it comes from σ P GalpM|Lq
by inverse of the natural projection prM so UL Ď GalpK|Lq. Conversely considering the projection
prM, prMpGalpK|Lqq Ď GalpM|Lq, so GalpK|Lq Ď pr´1
M pGalpM|Lqq “ UL. Making GalpK|Lq a
closed subgroup of G by Lemma 1.4.8.
Now let L|k be an arbitrary separable subextension (possibly infinite) of K|k. Then L|k can be
written as a union of finite separable subextensions Lα|k for which the Galois groups GalpLα|kq are
open in G so also closed as shown above. Since their intersection is precisely GalpK|Lq, we have
that GalpK|Lq is a closed subgroup of G.
In the opposite direction, let H be a closed subgroup of G. Then H is a subgroup of GalpK|Lq for
L “ KH. We are going the show the other inclusion: let σ P GalpK|Lq and pick a fundamental open
neighbourhood UM of the identity in GalpK|Lq (so the kernel of the projection prM), in fact it is also a
subgroup corresponding to a Galois extension M|L (UM “ GalpM|Lq). And H Ă GalpK|Lq surjects
into GalpM|Lq by prM |GalpK|Lq : GalpK|Lq Ñ GalpM|Lq (denote this restricted projection by prj).
Indeed, if prjMpHq Ĺ GalpM|Lq, then prjMpHq would be a subgroup fixing a field N “ MprjMpHq
with L Ă N Ă M. But this is a contradiction since each element of MzL is moved by an element
of H (recall it only fixes L), so prjMpHq “ GalpM|Lq. In particular some element in H must map
to the same element in GalpM|Lq by prjM as σ for example. Hence H contains an element of σUM
and since UM was chosen arbitrarily, σ is in the closure of H, but H is closed so σ P H and we have
proved that GalpK|Lq Ď H.

theory :

We can now start to show Grothendieck’s reformulation of Galois theory which focuses on the
absolute Galois group and sets equipped with a continuous and transitive action.
Definition 1.4.14. We define the separable closure of k in k as the compositum of all separable
extensions of k. And we call Galpkq “ Galpks|kq the absolute Galois group.
Remark 7. It is quite standard to show that the separable closure ks is a separable extension of k.
Indeed, if M and L are two separable extensions, we have that LM “ Mpα1, ..., αnq for α1, ...αn the
separable elements of L. Since the αi are separable over k they are separable over M, so LM|M is
a separable extension, as well as M|k by hypothesis. So by the property of tower extensions, LM|k
is a separable extension.

Fix a base field k and consider the separable closure ks.
Let L be a finite separable extension
of k.
From classical Galois theory, we know that L has finitely many k-homomorphisms into k
whose images are actually contained in ks.
So we may consider a finite set HomkpL, ksq (with
| HomkpL, ksq| “ rL : ks) and endow it with the action of Galpkq given by pg, ϕq ÞÑ g ˝ ϕ for
g P Galpkq and ϕ P HomkpL, ksq.
And from our previous discussion on topological spaces, we have the following result.
Corollary 1.4.15. The action of Galpkq on HomkpL, ksq as above is continuous and transitive.
Proof. The stabiliser U of an element ϕ P HomkpL, ksq consists of the elements of Galpkq fixing
ϕpLq.
And by the main theorem of Galois for infinite extensions U is open, hence Galpkq acts
continuously on HomkpL, ksq by the above lemma since HomkpL, ksq has the discrete topology. L|k
being separable, by the primitive element theorem, L is generated by a primitive element α with
minimal polynomial f. Each ϕ P HomkpL, ksq is given by mapping α to a root of f in ks. Since
Galpkq permutes these roots transitively , the Galpkq-action on HomkpL, ksq is transitive.
Corollary 1.4.16. Let L|k be a finite separable extension, then HomkpL, ksq as a Galpkq-set is
isomorphic to the left coset space of some open subgroup in Galpkq. If L|k is Galois, this coset space
is in fact a quotient by an open normal subgroup.
Proof. The first statement is immediate from the above corollary and Theorem A.2.1 aq with the
open subgroup being the stabiliser U of any element ϕ P HomkpL, ksq.
By the main theorem of Galois for infinite extensions, the coset U{ Galpkq becomes a quotient if and
only if U is a normal subgroup.
Before stating the reformulation of Galois theory by Grothendieck, we have to introduce the func-
tor responsible for the equivalence of categories.
This functor is given by L ÞÑ HomkpL, ksq
for L a finite separable extension of k and φ ÞÑ φ˚ for φ : L Ñ M a k-homomorphism and
φ˚ : HomkpM, ksq Ñ HomkpL, ksq a Galpkq-equivariant map defined by pre-composition with φ.
Thus the functor Homkp , ksq is a contravariant functor from the category FSepk of finite separable
extensions of k to the category Galpkq-FTCSet of finite sets equipped with a Galpkq transitive and
continuous action.
Theorem 1.4.17 (Reformulation of Galois Theory). Let k be a field with separable closure ks. The
above contravariant functor Homkp , ksq gives an anti-equivalence of categories between FSepk and
Galpkq-FTCSet. And Galois extensions give rise to Galpkq-sets isomorphic to some finite quotient
of Galpkq.

1.4.2
´Etale and Separable Algebras
We end this section with an important generalisation of the previous reformulation theorem, which
makes use of ´etale algebras, a natural replacement for finite separable extensions (for commutative
algebras). It allows one to consider sets with a continuous but not necessarily transitive Galpkq-
action.
Definition 1.4.18. A finite-dimensional commutative k-algebra A is said to be ´etale (over k) if it
is isomorphic to a finite direct product of separable extensions of k.
Remark 8. Morphisms of finite ´etale k-algebras are simply morphisms of k-algebras. From this
we can consider the category of finite ´etale k-algebras F´eAlk. And just as before, for A an ´etale
k-algebra, the Galpkq-action on the separable closure ks induces a left action on the set of k-algebra
homomorphisms HomkpA, ksq. Allowing us to consider the functor Homkp , ksq with domain F´eAl
Theorem 1.4.19. Let k be a field with separable closure ks. Then above functor Homkp, ksq mapping
a finite ´etale k-algebra A to the finite set HomkpA, ksq gives an anti-equivalence between the categories
F´eAlk and Galpkq-FCSet. Here separable field extensions give rise to sets with transitive Galpkq-
actions and Galois extensions to Galpkq-sets isomorphic to quotients of Galpkq.
Proof. This theorem follows from the previous one by noticing that, given a finite decomposition
A “ ś
i Li into a product of fields and an element ϕ P HomkpA, ksq, the maps ϕ induces the injection
of exactly one Li in ks. Indeed, if ϕpLiq ‰ 0, being a field extension, Li injects into ks, and on the
other hand, a product Li ˆ Lj cannot inject into ks since ks has no zero-divisors. Thus we have
the decomposition HomkpA, ksq – Ů

for each i, j. And these correspond bijectively to morphisms of the corresponding Galpkq-sets by the
previous theorem.
´Etale algebras will also play an important role later in this thesis. For that reason, we give another
characterisation of finite ´etale k-algebras. For that, we first need the next lemma.
Lemma 1.4.20. A finite-dimensional commutative algebra A over a field k is isomorphic to a direct
sum of finite field extensions of k if and only if A is reduced.
Proof. The “only if” part is obvious, one just has to notice that fields are already reduced.
To prove the other direction, by decomposing A into a finite direct product of indecomposable k-
algebras, we may assume that A is indecomposable. Under this restriction A can have no idempotent
elements other than 0 and 1, indeed, if e ‰ 0, 1 is an idempotent, then A – AeˆAp1´eq would be a
non-trivial direct product decomposition since ep1 ´ eq “ e ´ e2 “ 0. The lemma follows if we show
that every non-zero element x P A is invertible making it a field. Since A is finite-dimensional, it is
Artinian, i.e. the descending chain of ideals pxq Ą px2q Ą ... Ą pxnq Ą ... must stabilise. And thus
for some m, we have xn “ xn`1y for some y P A. By iteration we get xn “ xn`iyi for i P N until
xn “ x2nyn, thus pxnynq “ pxnynq2, an idempotent. If xnyn “ 0, then xn “ pxnqpxnynq “ 0 which
is a contradiction since x ‰ 0 by assumption. So xnyn “ 1 and x is invertible. A is reduced.

To see that equality holds, observe that HomkpA, kq – HomkpA bk k, kq are bijective as sets.

We have discussed so far a generalisation of separable field extension through ´etale algebras, and
seen that it allows to refine the classification field extensions. Another plausible and useful generali-
sations of separable field extension is the notion of separable algebras, it can be considered a broader
generalisation than the ´etale case since it allows to consider non-commutative algebras.
Definition 1.4.22. An algebra A over a field k is called separable if there exists a map ∆:
A bk A Ñ A with mp∆paqq “ a and a∆pbq “ ∆pabq “ ∆paqb, for a, b P A and m the usual
multiplication of A.
Proposition 1.4.23. Commutative separable algebras and finite ´etale algebras coincide over fields.
Proof. Corollary 4.5.8 in [For17] says that such a commutative k-algebra A is a separable algebra
if and only if A is isomorphic to a finite direct sum of fields L1 ‘ ... ‘ Ln where each Li is a finite
separable field extension of k. But this is exactly the definition of finite ´etale k-algebras.
For m : A bk A Ñ A the multiplication map of a k-algebra A, denote its kernel I. Notice that if A
is a commutative algebra, A bk A is a commutative algebra in its own right, and I is an ideal in this
algebra because it is an A,A-bimodule : for all x P I, pa b bqx “ axb P I for a, b P A.
Lemma 1.4.24. An algebra A over a commutative ring is separable if and only if this short exact
sequence of A,A-bimodules splits

Proof. We use the splitting lemma (in [Lan02], Proposition 3.2). We can define a map ∆: A Ñ AbA
so that m ˝ ∆“ idA. And ∆is an A,A-bimodule map if and only if a∆pbq “ ∆pabq “ ∆paqb. But
this is precisely the definition of a separable algebras.

1.5
Another Classification of Covering Spaces
Similarly as in Galois theory, there is a formulation “`a la Grothendieck” of the classification of
covering spaces in terms of equivalence of categories via a fibre functor.
Theorem 1.5.1. Let X be a connect topological space and x P X a base point. The fibre functor

We can state a refinement of the previous theorem. It applies the theorem to our previous discussion

Remark 9. Earlier, we have shown that Galois groups of Galois extensions are profinite, however
fundamental groups of topological spaces are generally not profinite; a prime example of this is the
fundamental group of the circle π1pS1q “ Z which cannot be seen as profinite.
Notice that this refinement is much closer to Grothendieck’s formulation of Galois theory for finite
field extensions (Theorem 1.4.17). Since the subsequent chapter will mostly focus on finite coverings
(over algebraic spaces), this refinement is quite fitting. The “new” tool we use here is the profinite
completion of groups defined in the previous section.
Definition 1.5.2. We say that a covering space p : Y Ñ X is finite if it has finite fibres. For a
connected topological space, these fibres have the same cardinality, called the degree of the covering.

1.6
Classification of Locally Constant Sheaves
The theories of locally constant sheaves and covering spaces are closely related. In this section,
we show that they are, in fact, equivalent. The reader may find all the necessary preliminaries to
sheaves in Appendix C.1. Let us now introduce the main objects of this section.
Definition 1.6.1. Let X be a topological space and S be a discrete set. We define a sheaf of sets
FS on X by taking FSpUq to be the set of continuous functions U Ñ S. But since S is discrete,
the sections of FS are just constant functions, thus FSpUq “ S and we call this sheaf the constant
sheaf with values in S. A sheaf F on X is then said to be locally constant if the restriction F|U
is a constant sheaf for all open U in some open covering of X.
We will study locally constant sheaves through the lens of another sheaf construction.
Construction 1.6.2. Given a morphism of topological spaces p : Y Ñ X, we define the presheaf
FY of sets on X, called the presheaf of sections of p as follows:

bq for any two open subsets V Ă U Ă X, the restriction map ρFY

Remark 10. A first interesting fact is that for sheaves of sections of covering maps, fibres and
stalks at a point x P X are the same. Indeed let ϕ : FY,x Ñ p´1pxq be the map that sends the
representative rssx to its image spxq. This map is well-defined since equivalent sections agree over
an open neighbourhood of x. When ϕprssxq “ ϕprtsxq in p´1pxq, since p is a local homeomorphism,
there exist an open neighbourhood V Ă X of x such that spV q “ tpV q, and since they are sections,
s “ t near x, so rssx “ rtsx. For any y P p´1pxq, we have that p|V : V Ñ U is a homeomorphism,
with s :“ pp|V q´1 a section such that ϕprssxq “ spxq “ y.
Proposition 1.6.3. The presheaf FY of sections of p is indeed a sheaf. Moreover, if p : Y Ñ X is
a covering, then FY is a locally constant sheaf. FY is a constant sheaf if and only if p : Y Ñ X is
a trivial cover.
Proof. Since sections are continuous maps over an open U Ă X, the glueing property of sheaves is
immediately verified. Suppose now that p : Y Ñ X is a covering. For any x P X take a connected
open neighbourhood V of x such that the cover p over V is trivial. So over V the cover is isomorphic
to V ˆS, for S the fibre at x. Let s P FY pV q be a local section of p over V . Its image is a connected

two different connected components of p´1pV q, sections of p over V correspond bijectively to points
of the fibre S. Thus the restriction FY |V is the constant sheaf with value S. And we get that FY
is a constant sheaf iff the connected open set V is the whole of X.
We can now define a functor from the category of covering spaces of X to the category of locally

Corollary 1.6.10. Let k be a field. The category of locally constant sheave of k-modules LCSk is
equivalent to the category of representations of π1pX, xq over k.
This equivalence is quite strong as it is also an equivalence that stands for the category of complex
local systems.
Definition 1.6.11. A complex local system on X is a locally constant sheaf of finite-dimensional
complex vector spaces. If X is connected, all stalks must have the same dimension, which is called
the dimension of the local system.
Hence the category of local systems LSX on X is equivalent to the category of locally constant sheaf
LCSCpXq. The following corollary, is known as the monodromy representation of local systems.
Corollary 1.6.12. Let X be a connected an locally simply-connected topological space with x P
X. The category of complex local systems on X is equivalent to the category of finite-dimensional
representations of π1pX, sq.
Proof. Follows from Theorem 1.6.8 and Corollary 1.6.10.
Local systems are a powerful tool from algebraic topology. They also arise naturally in differential
geometry when one tries computes solutions of linear differential equations as the following example
shows.
Example 1.6.13. Let D Ă C be a connected open disc. Consider over D homogeneous n-th order
linear differential equation
ypnq ` f1ypn´1q ` ... ` fn´1y1 ` fny “ 0
where the fi are holomorphic functions on D.
Consider the local holomorphic solutions of the
differential equation over each open subset U Ă D. As C-linear combinations of local solutions of
the differential equation over U are again local solutions, they form a C-vector space SpUq. And
due to a classical theorem from linear differential equations (Theorem 11.2 in [For81]), each point of
D has an open neighbourhood U where the C-vector space SpUq has a finite basis x1, ..., xn. This
actually makes the sets of local solutions of the differential equation a sheaf S Ă On, a sheaf of
complex vector spaces. And since the restriction of the above basis xi to smaller open sets still form
a basis for the solutions, we can conclude that the sheaf S is locally constant, hence a local system
of dimension n.
28


## Page 30


Remark 12. But local systems have a deeper connection to differential geometry. Notably, the
Riemann-Hilbert correspondence says that the category of holomorphic flat connections on a complex
manifold X is equivalent to the category of complex local systems on X.
And with the above equivalence, this correspondence partial answers the 21st Hilbert problem which
asks about the existence of a linear differential equation having a prescribed monodromy group.
29


## Page 31


2
Covers and Fundamental Groups for Schemes
In the last chapter, we discussed how the theory of covering spaces provided a nice geometric in-
terpretation of the fundamental group of topological spaces. In this chapter, we explain in what
manner(s) the Zariski topology is too coarse. Then we go through a study of ´etale morphisms in
order to define ´etale coverings, which will allow us to define the ´etale fundamental group similarly
as in the previous chapter, denoted by πe
1. Finally, we compare our new fundamental group with its
topological counterpart by analytifying non-singular projective schemes over C. We also present a
few structural properties of πe
1pXq.
2.1
The ´Etale Topology
As one knows, we usually endow an algebraically variety over an algebraic closed field or a scheme
with the Zariski topology. But this topology is not well-suited for the methods of algebraic topology.
In the 1940’s, Andr´e Weil conjectured that the number of solutions to a set of polynomials over
a finite field might be explained by a cohomology theory for which the Lefshetz formula for fixed
points would hold (roughly), giving motivation to the study of cohomology of algebraic spaces (see
Appendix C.4). But the cohomology groups computed using the Zariski topology (the Z-cohomology)
are all zero for an important class of spaces (this follows from Grothendieck’s vanishing theorem).
This later led to the definition of the ´etale topology of a scheme by Grothendieck, which in turn
gave birth to the theory of ´etale cohomology. It is thus quite awkward to motivate the need for the
´etale topology without mentioning strong ideas from cohomology (which fall outside the scope of
this thesis). Nonetheless, we will see that for our purposes, the Zariski topology is also ill-suited.
Theorem 2.1.1 (Grothendieck’s vanishing theorem). If X is an irreducible topological space, then
HrpX, Fq “ 0 for all constant sheaves and all r ą 0.
Proof. See [Mil25], Theorem 1.1.
And the ˇCech cohomology groups are also zero.
The Zariski topology on an irreducible scheme X is quite coarse; it has very few open sets which
are additionally dense, and all its closed sets are finite. From this only, we already understand that
some key notions of algebraic topology fail; it is impossible to “zoom in” on a point, and there is no
notion of a small open ball centred at a point that usual topology is based on.
Even better, we can show that a very wide range of irreducible varieties and in fact every irreducible
schemes are contractible (simply-connected) when endowed with the Zariski topology.
Definition 2.1.2. Let X be a topological space. A cofinite subset of a set X is a subset A whose
complement is a finite set. The cofinite topology is a topology whose open subsets are precisely
all cofinite subsets and the empty set.
It is immediately clear that in dimension 1, the Zariski topology is cofinite.
Lemma 2.1.3. Let X be a set of cardinality at least 2ℵ0 endowed with the cofinite topology. Then
X is contractible.
Proof. The assumption is introduced simply to require that |X| “ |r0, 1s| (in fact this forces X
to be path-connected). Now from [TZ82] §10.35 , we have the following result : |X ˆ r0, 1s| “
maxt|X|, |r0, 1s|u “ |X| (since they are both infinite sets). So, we may consider an injection f :
X ˆ r0, 1s ãÑ X.
Such an injection is continuous by the definition of the cofinite and product
topologies. Then for a fixed x0 P X, we define a homotopy from the constant map to the identity
map :

In set theory, the cardinality of the continuum is the cardinality of the set of real numbers R. It is
an infinite cardinal number and it is denoted as 2ℵ0, where ℵ0 represents the cardinality of the set
of natural numbers N.
Remark 13. Notice that it is not enough to assume that X is infinite. For example, the set of
rational numbers Q with the cofinite topology is not path-connected, which then implies that it
cannot be contractible.
Thus, an algebraic curve over an uncountable base field (e.g. C or even R) has trivial fundamental
groups. We can go even further, and claim that every irreducible schemes are contractible. To show
this, we give a preliminary result about irreducible schemes and generic points. They are points
that are non-necessarily closed and thus in the Zariski topology, their closure is the whole space.

While the Zariski topology is ideal in many cases, mainly for keeping track of algebraic data (such
as prime/maximal ideals, dimensionality, localisation, nilpotents, homomorphisms, ...) we have seen
that it fails at discerning geometric properties topologically. So, how to proceed from there ?
Since there is already a large theory of algebraic geometry built with the Zariski topology, it is rather
clever that we don’t discard all of it. To tackle this issue, Grothendieck had the brilliant idea to
consider not only open sets, but at the same time, open immersions. Thus, introducing a new kind
of topology (that can now even be defined on a whole category, see Appendix B.6). One can further
define the notion of a sheaf for a category.
In our case, recall that in the theory of coverings from algebraic topology, we are interested in
certain morphisms, those which are locally homeomorphic, so a good guess would be to consider
morphisms of schemes that “look like” local isomorphisms. We will see later that these are naturally
´etale morphisms. Although the ´etale topology is defined through the notion of ´etale morphisms, we
will not define what they are yet. For now, it is only important to keep in mind that they are the
algebraic analogue of local isomorphisms. All of this is explained in the next section.
Definition 2.1.6. Let X be a scheme.
We call an ´etale covering of X a family of morphisms
tfi : Xi Ñ XuiPI of schemes such that fi is ´etale for all i P I and X “ YifipXiq.
The ´etale topology is a Grothendieck topology on the category of schemes such that for X a
scheme, GpXq is an ´etale covering of X.

The category of schemes allows many different Grothendieck topologies for different purposes. In
this thesis, we will focus on the ´etale topology. To establish that it is indeed the correct notion, we
can compare the outcome ´etale results with results from the analytification of a scheme of finite type
over C (see Section 2.4 and Appendix C.5), which has the induced complex topology, a topology
standard in the context of algebraic topology and the fundamental groups. The initial comparison
theorem is the following, due to Serre and can be found in [Ser56] (Theorem 1, Chapitre 12).

2.2
On ´Etale Morphisms of Schemes
As motivated previously, to define a fundamental group over schemes, our first step will be to in-
troduce ´etale morphisms, the core of the ´etale topology. But before doing so, we need to introduce
important notions such as finiteness, flatness, sheaves of differentials and smoothness that are di-
rectly related to morphisms of schemes. Such notions are called relative notions. As Vakil suggests
in [Vak25], there are a gazillion finiteness conditions on morphisms of schemes. The ones we are
immediately interested in are (locally of) finite type, and (locally of) finite presentation.
2.2.1
Finite morphisms
In a vacuum, the following finiteness conditions ensure that the dimensions of the fibres of our
morphisms do not degenerate (that they stay finite).
Definition 2.2.1. We say that a morphism of schemes f : X Ñ Y is locally of finite type if
Y has an affine open covering tVi “ Spec Biu so that f ´1pViq has itself an affine open covering
tUij “ Spec Aiju with Aij finitely generated Bi-algebras.
Moreover, a morphism f is said to be of finite type, if in addition, each f´1pViq can be covered by
a finite number of Uij.
And a morphism f is said to be finite if each f ´1pViq “ Ui an open affine subscheme of X.
Remark 14. Usually it is require that smooth or ´etale morphism are not only locally of finite type,
but locally of finite presentation. A ring A is said to be a finitely presented B-algebra if
A – Brx1, ..., xns{pr1px1, ..., xnq, ..., rjpx1, ..., xnqq.
A morphism f : X Ñ Y is locally of finite presentation if for each affine open set Spec B of Y ,
f ´1pSpec Bq “ Yi Spec Ai with B Ñ Ai finitely presented.
Now notice that if we are in the Noetherian setting (so let B be Noetherian) then being finitely
presented is the same as being of finite type. And since we are restricting ourselves to the Noetherian
case, in the following we will not consider morphisms locally of finite presentation.
Examples 2.2.2.

is an affine scheme.

Proposition 2.2.4 (Composition). If f : X Ñ Y and g : Y Ñ Z are morphisms of schemes locally
of finite type, then g ˝ f : X Ñ Z is again locally of finite type.
Proof. Follows by transitivity of finite generation; if A is a finitely generated B- algebra and B is a
finitely generated C-algebra, then A is a finitely generated C-algebra.
Lemma 2.2.5. In the Noetherian setting, schemes of finite type over a field are of finite dimension.
Proof. Let X be a scheme of finite type over k, then X can be covered by affine sets Spec Ai where
the Ai are finitely generated k-algebras.
This means that each chain of closed subscheme with
the strict inclusion is finite, therefore the supremum of the length of chains of strictly increasing
irreducible closed sets of X is also finite.
Proposition 2.2.6. Let f : X Ñ Y be a morphism of schemes of finite type. Then for all q P Y ,
the fibre scheme Xy has finite dimension.
Proof. By base change, the fibre of a morphism of schemes of finite type is again of finite type over
the base change, which is now the residue field. Hence by the above lemma, its fibres at any point
are finite-dimensional.
Proposition 2.2.7. Let f : X Ñ Y be a finite morphism of Noetherian schemes. Then f is closed.
Proof. Since f is finite, locally, consider Y “ Spec B and X “ Spec A with A a finitely generated
B-algebra. Pick generators a1, ..., an P A and define a surjection Brx1, ..., xns Ñ A, by xi ÞÑ ai.
So we can consider the following embedding Spec A ãÑ Proj Brx0, ..., xns “ Pn

Proposition 2.2.8. Closed immersions are finite

2.2.2
Flat Morphisms
In [Mum88], David Mumford begins his section on flat morphisms with the following quote: The
concept of flatness is a riddle that comes out of algebra, but which technically is the answer to many
prayers.
And indeed, flatness is quite an intuitive notion in algebra while being quite mysterious in the
geometric realm. At the level of modules, flatness is a property about the extension of scalar functor.
In broad terms, it claims that the tensor product behaves nicely. Now, at the level of schemes, it is
again a relative notion that encapsulates the concept of a “continuous family of schemes”. By that
we mean that the fibres of a flat morphism of schemes does not vary too wildly.
Definition 2.2.9. An R-module M is called flat if tensoring with M over R as a functor :
´ bR M : ModR Ñ ModR1 is an exact functor. A morphism of rings f : B Ñ A is said to be flat if
A is flat as a B-module.
An additive functor F : ModR Ñ ModR1 is called an exact functor if it sends (short) exact sequences
of R-modules to (short) exact sequences of R1-modules. We make the distinction that a functor F
is left exact if it sends a short exact sequence 0 Ñ N Ñ N 1 Ñ N 2 Ñ 0 to a sequence that is exact
at FpNq and FpN 1q. Conversely, F is right exact if it sends the short exact sequence above to a
sequence exact at FpN 1q and FpN 2q.
33


## Page 35


Remark 15. Note that the functor ´ bR M : ModR Ñ ModR is always right exact; the extension
of scalar functor is a left adjoint of the restriction of scalar functor. Left adjoint functors preserve
all colimits, and in particular they preserve cokernels (much like kernels detect injectivity by trivial
kernel, a map is surjective if and only if its cokernel is trivial). So the extension of scalar functor
actually preserves surjectivity and thus is a right exact functor. Hence, to show the exactness of the
extension of scalar functor it is enough to show only left exactness (that it preserves injections).
We now show a few important properties of flatness, such as compatibility with base change and
localisation, which are crucial in algebraic geometry to make sense of pulling back structures and
the interplay between local and global settings.
Proposition 2.2.10. Projective modules are flat.

Remark 16. Any scheme X over a field k is flat. If k is a field, then any k-module is actually a
free module, hence a flat module. Schematically, we have that X is flat at a point x if OX,x is flat,
and since it is a k-algebra, it is then flat by the preceding argument.
This can be understood geometrically as well, flatness means that the fibres of a morphism X Ñ
Spec k “vary continuously”, and Spec k being a single point, it is obviously the case.

Example 2.2.20. Open embeddings are flat:
Let f : X Ñ Y be an open embedding of schemes. This means that X is isomorphic to an open
subscheme U of Y (that X and U are homeomorphic and that the induced map f# : OY |U Ñ f˚OX
is an isomorphism). From this we have that maps between stalks are isomorphisms as well and so
at any point of X, our map f is flat.
The next class of morphism of schemes is a weaker kind of flat morphisms.
Definition 2.2.21. A morphism f : X Ñ Y is said to be locally free if the direct image sheaf
f˚OX is a locally free as an OY -module.
Remark 17. It is easy and useful to see that locally free morphisms are flat. Indeed if f˚OX is
locally free as an OY -module, then locally on Y , f˚OX is free, hence flat by Corollary 2.2.11. Thus
f is now a flat morphism.
On the other hand, the converse is also true in the Noetherian setting. Recall that in this case
morphisms of finite type are equivalently of finite presentations (Remark 14), then by Corollary
24.4.7 in [Vak25], flatness and locally freeness are the same thing.
Proposition 2.2.22 (Composition). The composition of two locally free morphisms of schemes is
again locally free.
Proof. Let f : X Ñ Y and g : Y Ñ Z be locally free morphisms.
Then f˚OX is locally free
as a OY -module.

morphism.

2.2.3
The Sheaf of K¨ahler Differentials
The motivation behind K¨ahler differentials is to generalise the intuitive concept of differential forms
from differential geometry. One important aspect of the sheaf of K¨ahler differentials is that it is a
quasi-coherent sheaf whose fibre at any point is precisely the dual of the tangent space at that point.
It will also be central in the definition of smooth and ´etale morphisms. There are several ways of
defining the module of K¨ahler differentials, one of them is by means of derivations.
Definition 2.2.25. Suppose A is a B-algebra. We define a B-linear derivation on A to be a B-
module homomorphism d : A Ñ M to an A-module M satisfying the Leibniz rule : dpfgq “ fdg`gdf.
We denote the set of such B-linear derivations by DerBpA, Mq
Definition 2.2.26. The module of K¨ahler differentials is defined to be the A-module ΩA{B for
which there is a universal derivation d : A Ñ ΩA{B. The composition with d provides, for every
A-module M an A-module isomorphism HomApΩA{B, Mq –
ÝÑ DerBpA, Mq.
Now one way to construct ΩA{B and d is by constructing a free A-module as linear combinations of
the symbol da for each a P A and imposing the following relations :
• da ` da1 “ dpa ` a1q @a1 P A (additivity),
• dpaa1q “ ada1 ` a1da (Leibniz law) , and
• db “ 0 @b P B (triviality of pull-back).
Notice that d : A Ñ ΩA{B is B-linear. And the universal derivation sends a to da.
From the Leibniz law of differentials, we can easily deduce the quotient law and the chain rule for
differentials.
Examples 2.2.27.

being no further relations among the differentials, ΩA{B “ Adx1 ‘ ... ‘ Adxn.
bq If A “ S´1B with S a multiplicative closed subset of B, then ΩA{B “ 0.
Any element of A can be written as a “

Since L|k is a separable extension, for all α P L, there exists a polynomial p P krxs such that
ppαq “ 0 and p1pαq ‰ 0. So by the chain rule we have 0 “ dpppαqq “ p1pαqdα, so dα “ 0 for all

In the last example the converse is also true and it can be shown using separable algebras. But
before we need the following lemma :
Lemma 2.2.28. If a finitely generated ideal I in a commutative ring R has I2 “ I, then I “ pR
for p P R an idempotent.
Proof. The lemma is almost immediate following Nakayama’s Lemma A.0.3. We apply it to M “ I
and we get that there exist i P I such that im “ m for all m P I. It follows that i2 “ i and I “ iR,
and just let p “ i.
Proposition 2.2.29. Let A be a commutative k-algebra. If ΩA{k “ 0 then A is a separable k-algebra.
Proof. We show this later on (Theorem 2.2.35) but, ΩA{k “ 0 implies that I “ I2 for I the kernel of
δ which is the multiplication map for our k-algebra A. When k is a field and A is finite-dimensional,
notice that every ideal of A b A is finitely generated, so we can apply the above lemma and consider
the multiplication by the idempotent p for p P A b A such that I “ p ¨ pA b Aq. This gives an
A,A-bimodule map ¨ p : A b A Ñ I and we have p¨ pq ˝ i “ idI so by the splitting lemma, the exact
sequence in Lemma 1.4.24 splits making A separable.

So by combining these results, and remembering that separable commutative algebras coincide with

separable.
