2.2.4
Regular Schemes and Smooth Morphisms
Smoothness is a central and intuitive notion in geometry. In algebraic geometry, this notion is called
regularity and it is closely related to the dimension of the tangent space. We begin by defining the
tangent space of a scheme at a point and convince ourselves that it is reasonably defined.
Definition 2.2.40. The Zariski cotangent space of a local ring pA, mq is the vector space m{m2
over the residue field A{m and its dual is the Zariski tangent space. Now if X is a scheme,
the Zariski cotangent space TpX˚ of X at p P X is the cotangent space of the local ring OX,p
(and similarly for the Zariski tangent space TpX). Elements of the cotangent space are called
differentials (yes, as defined previously) and elements of the tangent space are called tangent vectors.
One way to see that this is the right definition is the following:
Construction 2.2.41. Since we have defined tangent spaces on varieties, let X be a variety and
p “ 0 be the origin in X corresponding to the maximal ideal m Ĳ ApXq. Consider the k-linear map
φ : m Ñ HomkpTpX, kq ; f ÞÑ df|TpX.
By definition of the tangent space, this map is well-defined and surjective since linear maps on TpX
must be combinations of the coordinate functions dx1, ..., dxn We now show that ker φ “ m2.
Notice that W :“ tdg | g P IpXqu is a linear subspace of krx1, ..., xns of dimension t, and its zero locus
TpX has dimension n ´ t. So the space of linear forms vanishing on TpX, (i.e. tdg | dg|TpX “ 0u)
has again dimension t and also contains W. Thus W “ tdg | dg|TpX “ 0u.
Now if f P ker φ, we know that df P W, so there exists a polynomial g P IpXq such that df “ dg,
where dg is a linear combination of the linear forms defining W. So f ´ g contains no constant term
(we are at the origin) and no linear term. This means exactly that f ´ g P m2. Notice here that
we have first understood f P ker φ as the restriction of a formal polynomial, defining a variety. We
managed to say a lot of things about f as a formal polynomial and ended up with two polynomials
f and g that are equal when restricted to X, hence we could write that f “ f ´ g with f on the
left hand side being the restriction of f to X. While this is confusing, we rather explain it this way
than to introduce new notation.
And since m2 is generated by products fg for f, g P m, we have φpfgq “ fppqdg|TpX `gppqdf|TpX “ 0
since fppq “ gppq “ 0.
By the isomorphism theorem, we can conclude that indeed m{m2 – TpX˚ (the dual of TpX).
We are now able to state the definition of regularity for local rings,
40


## Page 42


Definition 2.2.42. We say that a local ring pR, mq is regular if the dimension of the local ring is
equal to the dimension of the Zariski cotangent space, i.e. dim R “ dimκ m
L
m2, with κ the residue
field R{m. A scheme X is called regular at a point p if the local ring OX,p is regular. Otherwise,
X is said to be singular at p.
Recall that the sheaf of K¨ahler differentials encodes the data of tangent spaces of a scheme over a
base scheme. Then it would make sense to characterise regularity of a scheme through its sheaf of
K¨ahler differentials.
Proposition 2.2.43. Let X be an irreducible scheme of finite type over k an algebraically closed
scheme. Then ΩX{k is a locally free sheaf of rank n if and only if X is a regular scheme over k.
Proof. See [Har83] Theorem 8.15.
The following result gives us a necessary condition for regularity.
Theorem 2.2.44 (Auslander–Buchsbaum). Any regular local ring is a unique factorisation domain
(UFD).
Proof. See [AB59].
And since, by definition, any UFD is an integral domain, regular local rings are integral domains.
Then any point where 2 irreducible components of a variety meet must be a singular point.
Remark 18. From commutative algebra (Proposition 4.20 in [Eis95]), we have that UFD’s are
normal rings. So for a scheme X, regularity at P implies normality, i.e. we say that a scheme X is
normal at P if the local ring OX,P is normal: an integrally closed integral domain.
But for schemes of degree 1, so curves, the other implication is also true. Indeed, normal local rings
are actually also discrete valuation rings (DVR) and in dimension 1, regularity for a local ring is
equivalent to being a DVR (again, see 11.1, 11.2 [Eis95]). This means that for curves, regularity and
smoothness are the same.
Our next concern is that regularity does not refer to a base scheme. For this reason, we introduce the
notion of smooth morphisms of schemes and say that a scheme X is smooth over k if X Ñ Spec k
is a smooth morphism. One misconception is that smooth morphisms in algebraic geometry are
the counterpart of smooth morphisms from differential geometry. This is not the case, the correct
picture is the one below,
Submersions ÐÑ Smooth morphisms
Local Isomorphism ÐÑ ´Etale morphisms
Immersions ÐÑ Unramified morphisms
(2)
As a quick reminder, in differential geometry, a submersion is a smooth map of manifolds f : M Ñ N
whose differential Dfp : TpM Ñ TfppqN is surjective at any point p P M. An immersion is similar
but requires that the differential is injective at any points. And a smooth map is called a local
isomorphism if it is an immersion and a submersion at the same time.
Definition 2.2.45. A morphism of schemes f : X Ñ Y is smooth of relative dimension n if it
is flat, locally of finite type and ΩX{Y is locally free of rank n.
Here, of relative dimension n only means that all non-empty fibres have the same dimension n.
Flatness ensures that the fibres of the morphism are of the same dimension, and ΩX{Y is locally
free of rank n specifies what the dimensions of those fibres are; this last condition is not surprising
considering Proposition 2.2.43. Again, smooth morphisms of schemes are also compatible with base
change and composition.
Proposition 2.2.46 (Base change). Let f : X Ñ Y be a smooth morphisms of schemes of relative
dimension n and consider the following fibre product diagram for any morphism g : Y 1 Ñ Y with
X1 “ X ˆY Y 1, Then the morphism f 1 : X1 Ñ Y 1 is also smooth of relative dimension n.
Proof. By Proposition 2.2.17, 2.2.3 and 2.2.38 f1 is smooth again.

Proposition 2.2.47 (Composition). If f : X Ñ Y is smooth of relative dimension n, and g : Y Ñ Z
is smooth of relative dimension m, then g ˝ f : X Ñ Z is smooth of relative dimension n ` m.
Proof. See [Har83], Proposition 10.1. pcq.
Remark 19. A nice way to see that smooth morphisms are indeed linked to submersions is by
reasoning as follows, let f : X Ñ Y be a smooth morphism of regular schemes of relative dimension
n “ dim X ´ dim Y over k an algebraically closed field. By Proposition 2.2.39, we get the exact

f ˚ΩY {k Ñ ΩX{k Ñ ΩX{Y Ñ 0.

f ˚ΩY {k b κpPq
ϕÝÑ ΩX{k b κpPq Ñ ΩX{Y b κpPq Ñ 0.
Recall that since X and Y are regular schemes over k and that ΩX{Y is locally free of rank n, the
now vector spaces above, have dimensions dim Y , dim X and n respectively. By right-exactness, we

is actually surjective.
2.2.5
´Etale Morphisms
Finally, we arrive at the main definitions of this section.
Definition 2.2.48. A morphism of schemes f : X Ñ Y is said to be ´etale if it is flat, locally of
finite type and ΩX{Y “ 0.
Notice the resemblance to the definition of smoothness for morphisms of schemes. The only difference
being that the sheaf of relative differential need not only to be locally free, but it has to be the zero
sheaf. This can be interpreted as a “hidden” condition for ´etaleness, requiring that the morphism
must be at the same time smooth and unramified (see relation picture (2))
Definition 2.2.49. Let f : X Ñ Y be a morphism of schemes locally of finite type, and P P X.
f is said to be unramified at P if the residue field of X at P is a separable field extension of the
residue field of Y at fpPq. And f#pmfpP qqOX,P “ mP for mP and mfpP q are local rings of the local
rings OX,P and OY,fpP q.
We mention the following characterisation of unramified morphism through fibres.
Proposition 2.2.50. Let f : X Ñ Y be a morphisms of finite type, then the following are equivalent:
aq f is unramified
bq for all Q P Y , the fibre XQ Ñ Spec κpQq over X is unramified.
cq for all Q P Y , the fibre XQ is the spectra of a finite separable κpQq-algebras.
dq for all Q P Y , the fibre XQ is a finite disjoint union of Spec ki, where ki are finite separable
field extensions of κpQq (or the spectrum of a finite ´etale κpsq-algebra).
Proof. See [Mil25], Proposition 3.2.
Proposition 2.2.51. A morphism of schemes f : X Ñ Y locally of finite type is said to be unram-
ified at P P X if and only ΩX{Y “ 0 at P.
Proof. See [Mil25] Proosition 3.5.
Thus, from Example 2.2.37, we have that open embeddings are unramified (in fact, any immersion is
unramified) and with the previous proposition, we can reformulate our definition for ´etale morphisms
of schemes.
42


## Page 44


Definition 2.2.52. A morphism of schemes f : X Ñ Y is said to be ´etale if it is equivalently flat
and unramified.
From this definition, it is clear that ´etale morphisms must behave like local isomorphisms, being at
the same times submersions and immersions.
Examples 2.2.53. From previous examples, open embeddings are ´etale.
Proposition 2.2.54 (Base change). Let f : X Ñ Y be an ´etale morphism of schemes and consider
f 1 : X1 Ñ Y 1 the base change of f. Then f 1 is ´etale again.
Proof. Since smooth morphisms are stable under base change (2.2.46), we need to show that unram-
ified morphisms are stable under base change. But they clearly are since by Proposition 2.2.38 and
by the fact that the pull-back of a zero sheaf is again a zero sheaf.
Proposition 2.2.55 (Composition). Let f : X Ñ Y and g : Y Ñ Z be two ´etale morphisms of
schemes. Then g ˝ f : X Ñ Z is also ´etale.
Proof. By Propositions 2.2.4, 2.2.18 and since ΩX{Y and f ˚ΩY {Z are zero sheave, by Proposition
2.2.39, ΩX{Z “ 0.
Proposition 2.2.56. Let f : X Ñ Y be a finite ´etale morphism,. Then the diagonal morphism
∆: X Ñ XˆY X coming from f is an open and closed immersion (in particular ∆is an isomorphism
of X onto its image).
Proof. Since f is finite, it is separated and hence the diagonal morphism ∆: X Ñ X ˆY X is a
closed immersion, it is left to show that it is an open map. Consider U an open subscheme of X ˆY X
such that ∆: Y Ñ U is a still a closed immersion. Let I be the sheaf of ideals on X ˆY X, then
restricted to ∆pXq, the quotient I{I2 – ΩX{Y .
Since we are in the Noetherian setting, X ˆY X is Noetherian, making the ideal sheaf I a coherent
sheaf, so we can apply Nakayama’s lemma A.0.3. Thus obtaining that I “ 0 on an open subset U
of X ˆY X, restricting the closed immersion ∆pXq ãÑ X ˆY X to an isomorphism ∆pXq – U. So
∆pXq is both open and closed in X ˆY X.
Notice in this case, X is isomorphic to a union of connected component of X ˆY X.
Example 2.2.57. A morphism of affine schemes Spec B Ñ Spec A is finite ´etale if and only if B is
a finite projective A-module and for every point P P Spec A, B bA κpPq is a finite separable algebra
over the residue field.

2.3
Finite ´Etale Covers and Algebraic Fundamental Groups of Schemes
Remember that we are forgoing a Noetherian investigation; all our rings and schemes are to be
Noetherian. In that case, we may replace flat by locally free and we can restrict to working with
morphisms of finite type. Every scheme is defined over a base scheme S. And since ´etale morphisms
are insensitive to base change, we can consider the geometric fibres instead of the classical fibres.
One reason to consider such geometric fibres is the following construction.
43


## Page 45


Construction 2.3.1. Let f : X Ñ S be a finite ´etale morphism of schemes. A geometric point s
on S is a morphism of schemes s : Spec Λ Ñ S, such that the image of s is a point s P S and where Λ
is an algebraically closed field extension of the residue field κpsq. The geometric fibre Xs of f over s
is defined as the fibre product XˆSSpec Λ. It is often more useful to work with geometric fibres since
it results in working with schemes defined over algebraically closed fields. Using Theorem 1.4.21, we
can actually conclude that the geometric fibre Xs has finitely many points. Indeed if S “ Spec A
and X “ Spec B locally with B a finite A-algebra, then Xs “ Spec pB bA Λq with B bA Λ a finite
(n-)dimensional Λ-algebra, invoking Theorem 1.4.21 we obtain that B bA Λ – Λ ˆ ... ˆ Λ (n times).
Therefore Xs “ Spec Λ \ ... \ Spec Λ, a scheme consisting of n points.
So, in light of Proposition 2.2.50 and Corollary 2.2.52, we have an analogue definition.
Definition 2.3.2. A finite morphisms of schemes f : X Ñ S is said to be finite ´etale if it is locally
free (flat) and if each fibre Xs of f is the spectrum of a finite ´etale κpsq-algebra (unramified).
Notice from Propositions 2.2.7 and 2.2.19, that the image of a finite ´etale morphisms is both open
and closed.
Remark 20. Resuming our discussion over geometric fibres, we get from Theorem 1.4.21 that the
fibres Xs are spectra of a finite ´etale κpsq-algebra if and only if its geometric fibres are of the form
SpecpΛˆ...ˆΛq, or in other words that they are finite disjoint union of points defined over Λ (when
f is a finite ´etale morphism). This is the main reason behind our preference for the geometric fibres.
In the following, we provide a theory of Galois “`a la Grothendieck” for finite ´etale covers analogous
to the theory developed for the classification of field extensions and of covering spaces in the first
chapter.
2.3.1
Finite ´Etale Coverings
Definition 2.3.3. A morphism f : X Ñ S of schemes is a finite ´etale cover if f is a surjective
finite ´etale morphism. And f is called a trivial finite ´etale cover if X as a scheme over S is
isomorphic to a disjoint union of copies of S, and the map f restricts to identity on each component.
Example 2.3.4. The simplest case would be to check how finite ´etale coverings would look like
over S “ Spec k for k an algebraically closed field. Let us consider X any finite ´etale covering
over S. Since f is finite and ´etale, we know that X is the spectrum of a finite ´etale k-algebra A.
And by Theorem 1.4.21, we have that A – A bk k – k‘n because k is algebraically closed. So
X “ Spec k \ ... \ Spec k (n times), meaning that Spec k only has trivial finite ´etale covers which
makes sense since S is just a point.
In order to make more sense of trivial coverings, we show that finite ´etale coverings are locally trivial,
we want the situation to be analogous to that of topological coverings. Notice that the restriction of
a cover Y Ñ X above an open subset U Ă X is none but the fibre product Y ˆX U, the proposition
below then proves our claim.
Proposition 2.3.5. Let f : X Ñ S be an affine surjective morphism of schemes with S connected.
Then f is a finite ´etale cover if and only if, there is a finite, locally free and surjective morphism
g : Y Ñ S such that X ˆS Y is a trivial cover of Y .
Proof. Suppose that g is finite, locally free and surjective and X ˆS Y is a trivial cover of Y . As
g is locally free, each point in S is contained in an affine open subset U “ Spec A over which g
restricts to a morphism Spec C Ñ Spec A with C an A-algebra that is finitely generated and free
as an A-module. If f restricts to Spec B Ñ Spec A over U, then we have the following base change
SpecpB bA Cq Ñ Spec B where B bA C is a finitely generated free C-module, hence also a finitely
generated free A-module. On the other hand, SpecpB bA Cq is isomorphic to a finite direct sum of
Spec B since it is a trivial cover, and this is only possible if B is finitely generated free A-module.
This shows that f must be finite and locally free. Let s : Spec Λ Ñ Y be a geometric point of Y .
By composition with g it yields a geometric point of S. The geometric fibres Xps˝gq :“ X ˆS Spec Λ
and pX ˆS Y qs :“ X ˆS Y ˆY Spec Λ are isomorphic and the latter is a finite direct sum of copies
of Spec Λ since X ˆS Y is a finite ´etale covering of Y . And since g is surjective, we have that all the
44


## Page 46


fibres of f are indeed finite ´etale κpsq-algebras (invoking Theorem 1.4.21). Thus f is a finite ´etale
cover.
In the other direction, suppose that f is a finite ´etale cover. S being a connected scheme, then all
fibres of f have the same cardinality say n. We now proceed by induction on n.
If n “ 1, then since f is finite ´etale, it is also an isomorphism. So it is possible to find a finite locally
free surjective morphism g : Y Ñ S such that S ˆS Y is a trivial cover of Y .
For n ą 1, we consider the base change XˆS X Ñ X (notice that pXˆS Xqx – Xs for x a point lying
above s, so |pXˆS Xqx| “ n for all x P X as well), by Proposition 2.2.56, the diagonal map ∆induces
a section of the base change map. In fact, X ˆS X is the disjoint union of ∆pXq with some open
and closed subscheme X1 of X, with X1 being the complement of ∆pXq. Now consider the inclusion
map X1 Ñ X ˆS X and the projection map X ˆS X Ñ X, these maps are both finite ´etale since
respectively, open embeddings are ´etale and finite ´etale morphisms are preserved under base change.
Then, by Proposition 2.2.55, the composition X1 Ñ X is also finite ´etale. By construction, we have
that the fibres of this morphism has cardinality n ´ 1. Indeed, since X ˆS X “ ∆pXq \ X1, we also
have that pX ˆS Xqx “ ∆pXqx \ X1

Definition 2.3.6. We can actually build a category whose objects consists of finite ´etale covering
X Ñ S over a base scheme S and whose morphisms are morphisms of schemes that are compatible
over S. It is a full subcategory of the category of relative schemes over S and we denote it F´etS.
Proposition 2.3.7. Let S be a connected scheme and p : X Ñ S is a morphisms in F´etS. If S is
a reduced scheme, then X is a reduced scheme as well.
Proof. We may assume that X “ Spec B and S “ Spec A. Let P be a point of S (a prime ideal).
By assumption, the localisation AP has no nilpotents and we will prove that the same holds for
CP “ B bA AP . This is sufficient since the local ring of points of X lying above P are localisations
of CP and p is surjective.
Let P1, ..Pr be the set of minimal prime ideals in AP . Their intersection is the nilradical (see [Eis95]
Corollary 2.12), so the ideal of nilpotents in AP , then the natural map AP Ñ śpAP {Piq is injective.
Now, since p is a finite ´etale cover, it is also locally free, so CP is a free AP -module. So tensoring
the above map with CP , we obtain an injective map CP Ñ śpCP {PiCP q.
Each Spec CP {PiCP is finite ´etale over Spec AP {Pi (by base change). So by considering this map,
we can reduce to the case where AP is an integral domain. Denoting by KP its fraction field, we get
that the following map Spec CP bAP KP Ñ Spec KP is finite ´etale cover by base change. And the
natural map KP Ñ CP bAP KP induced by the inclusion AP Ñ KP is injective since p is locally
free. So CP bAP KP is a finite product of fields (by ´etaleness) which is clearly reduced (reduced
rings are stable under finite product), hence CP ha no nilpotents.
From now on, the reader might notice the resemblance of certain of the following results with lemmas
and propositions from Section 1.2 and the theory of field extensions.
Lemma 2.3.8. Let f : X Ñ Y and g : Z Ñ X be morphisms of schemes. If f ˝ g and f are finite
´etale, then so is g.
Proof. By Proposition 2.2.56, the diagonal map X Ñ X ˆS X is finite as it is a closed immersion and
since X is isomorphic to a union of connected components in X ˆS X it is also ´etale. Considering
the fibre product of Z over X with Y via g, we get a morphism Γg : Z Ñ Z ˆY X (the ”graph” of
g) which is again finite and ´etale. The projection p2 : Z ˆY X Ñ X is again finite ´etale (being the
base change of f ˝ g : Z Ñ Y ). So the composition p2 ˝ Γg “ g is finite ´etale.
Proposition 2.3.9. Let f : X Ñ S be a morphism in F´etS, and s : S Ñ X be a section of f. Then
s induces an isomorphism of S with an open and closed subscheme of X.
Proof. By Lemma 2.3.8, s is a finite ´etale morphism. Now since s is finite, it is closed (2.2.7), so
its image is closed as well. Since s is flat and locally of finite type, the image of s is open as well
(2.2.19) And since sections are injective morphisms, the proof is done.

In particular, if S is connected, then the section s maps S isomorphically onto a whole connected

recall, it as used profusely throughout the first chapter.
Proposition 2.3.10. Let g : Z Ñ S be a morphism of schemes with S a connected scheme and
h1, h2 : Z Ñ X are two morphisms of schemes over S, with f : X Ñ S an ´etale morphism with
h1 ˝ z “ h2 ˝ z for some geometric point z : Spec Λ Ñ Z. Then h1 “ h2.
Proof. Consider the fibre product Z ˆS X, since ´etaleness is preserved under base change, we may
assume that Z “ S. Now, morphisms h1 and h2 become sections of f, so it is enough to check that
if two sections of a finite ´etale cover of a connected scheme S coincide at a geometric point, then
they are equal. This follows from Proposition 2.3.9 since each section is an isomorphism of (now) S
onto a connected component of X, thus it is determined by the image of a geometric point.
For a morphism of schemes f : X Ñ S, we define the group of scheme automorphisms of X preserving

Xs “ X ˆS Spec Λ (coming from the base change of its action on X).

fixed points on each geometric fibre. Hence AutpX|Sq is finite.
Proof. Just as in the proof of Corollary 1.2.12, the first statement is immediate from Proposition

of AutpX|Sq on the underlying set of geometric fibres is faithful. And since geometric fibres of finite
´etale morphisms are finite sets, and AutpX|Sq acts faithfully on such fibres, then AutpX|Sq is a
subgroup of SympXsq a finite group.

Before stating the analogue to the theorem of Galois classification of topological covers, we need to

cover or a Galois object if AutpX|Sq acts transitively on the underlying set of geometric fibres.

Remark 22. The case of affine group schemes is even more interesting, in the following we show

But this is exactly the structure of a Hopf algebra (see Definition 3.1.4). The construction being

We have defined group schemes over S as group objects from the category of schemes over S. But
sometimes this definition is not practical, for example, to define a group scheme one would have to
give a scheme G, then one needs to define the morphisms m, i and e and finally that they respect
the commutative diagrams. There is an incentive to describe a group scheme as a scheme whose
points form a group, it can be done as follows:
Construction 2.3.21. Suppose we have a scheme X over S. The notion of points of X is not as
transparent as in the case of sets or topological spaces. If T is a scheme over S, then we define a
T-valued point of X, as a morphism x : T Ñ X over S and denote the set of such points by XpTq.
In the particular case where S “ Spec k and T : Spec K where K|k is a field extension, we refer to
such points of X as K-rational points.
Definition 2.3.22. A functor F : C Ñ Grp is said to be a representable group-valued functor if
and only if the functor U˝F : C Ñ Set is representable where U : Grp Ñ Set is the forgetful functor.
Now the category of representable group-valued functors and natural transformations between them
is denoted as rC, Grps
The following result is quite powerful. It says that we can understand group schemes as functors and
it concludes our above construction by endowing spaces of the form XpTq with a group structure
for X and T schemes over S.
Proposition 2.3.23. There is an equivalence between the category of group schemes over S and the
category rSchS, Grps of representable group-valued functors from SchS to Grp.
Proof. Consider the category SchS of schemes over S and write C “ FunpSchS, Setq the cate-
gory of representable functors. Now for any scheme X P SchS, we define the representable functor
hX “ HomSchSp , Xq in C. Sending X to hX gives a covariant functor h : SchS Ñ C that is moreover
fully faithful (it is actually a Yoneda embedding, see Proposition B.1.9). So by the Yoneda lemma,
any X P SchS is uniquely determined by a representable functor F P C of the form by being its
unique representative up to isomorphism.
We can now define group objects in SchS via the above embedding h. Let X P SchS, to give a
group law on an object X means that for each scheme T over S, we have to specify a group law on
the set hXpTq “ HomSchSpT, Xq “ XpTq such that for every morphism f : T1 Ñ T2, the induced
map hXpfq : XpT2q Ñ XpT1q is a homomorphism of groups. Note that a category allows group
objects if it has a terminal object and finite product, which is the case for SchS (a terminal object
is the scheme S and fibre products of schemes over S are finite products).
So we can now define a functor phG “ HomSchSp , Gq : Sch Ñ Grp sending any scheme T over S
to the group phGpTq “ GpTq, by the above discussion this is a representable group-valued functor.
And it gives rise to the functor ph from the category of group schemes over S to the category of
representable group-valued functors by sending G ÞÑ hG.
We now establish the equivalence :
Given a group scheme pG, m, e, iq over S, its group axioms ensure that for every scheme T P SchS,
the set GpTq inherits a group structure as above, making the functor phG a representable group-
valued functor. Conversely, given a representable group-valued functor phG, the group structure on
phGpTq defines natural transformations pmG : phG ˆ phG Ñ phG, peG : phS Ñ phG and piG : phG Ñ phG.
And the Yoneda lemma tells us that these natural transformations correspond uniquely to S-
morphisms mG : G ˆS G Ñ G, eG : S Ñ G and iG : G Ñ G. Moreover, because the embedding
is fully faithful, the above natural transformations induced by group axioms over phGpTq for any
schemes T over S translate directly into the commutative diagrams required for G to be a group
scheme.
Remark 23. Since the category of affine schemes is anti-equivalent to that of commutative k-
algebras via the Spec functor, from the above proposition, an affine group schemes G over k is
equivalent to a group-valued representable functor from the category of k-algebras to the category
of groups, represented by some k-algebra A called the coordinate ring of G.

Recall from algebraic geometry that there is an anti-equivalence between the category of affine
schemes over k and the category of commutative k-algebras. We can then restrict the above equiva-
lence by an equivalence between affine group schemes over k and representable group-valued functors
on Algk. We continue by studying a few examples of group schemes.

In light of Theorem 1.4.19, we can ask ourselves if an equivalence of this type exists for finite ´etale
group schemes over a field k. The next lemma is necessary to answer this question. It is actually a
result from Galois descent theory, and is due to Andreas Speiser (it can be seen as a reinterpretation
of Hilbert’s Theorem 90). For more on Galois descent, see [GS17] § 2.3.
Lemma 2.3.25. Let L|k be a Galois extension with Galois group G “ GalpL|kq. Given a relative
Galois representation V
V – L b V G
Proof. See [GS17], Lemma 2.3.8.
Proposition 2.3.26. The category of finite ´etale group schemes over k is equivalent to the category
of finite groups carrying a continuous Galpkq-action.
Proof. Assume the base field S “ Spec k for k a field. Then by Theorem 1.4.21, a finite ´etale group
scheme G Ñ S is of the form G “ Spec A with a A finite ´etale k-algebra.
And by Theorem 1.4.19, it corresponds to a finite set equipped with a continuous Galpkq-action.
That finite set being the fibre Gks of G over the geometric point Spec ks Ñ G, it carries a group
structure coming from that of G, meaning it is compatible with the Galois action.
Conversely, the Galois-equivariant group operations on a finite continuous Galpkq-set, yield a group
scheme structure on the corresponding finite ´etale scheme over k.
Over ks, the corresponding
constant group of such a Galpkq-set X has a coordinate algebra ś
xPX ks. Naturally, it is isomorphic
to the vector space V “ tf : X Ñ ksu where an element a P ś ks is of the form pax1, ..., axnq and
is defined by a function fpxq “ ax. The absolute Galois group has an action over this vector space,
for any σ P Galpkq, we have σ ¨ paxqxPX “ pσpaσ´1xqqxPX. Notice that this equips V with a semi-
linear Galpkq-action. Now we can apply our above lemma for the descent argument. Consider the
subalgebra A “ V G consisting of all Galpkq-equivariant functions in V . Since all the requirements
of the lemma are verified, we have A bk ks – ś ks. From Theorem 1.4.21, this means that A is a
finite ´etale algebra, and so its spectrum gives a finite ´etale group scheme over Spec k.
To conclude that the resulting finite ´etale scheme admits a group structure making it a group scheme,
we are going to check that its coordinate ring A is a Hopf k-algebra.
We define a map ∆: A Ñ A bk A as follow, for an element f P A, we set ∆pfq a function on X ˆ X
to be ∆pfqpx, yq “ fpx¨yq. Since the group operation on X is compatible with the action of Galpkq,
for σ P Galpkq, we hve
σp∆pfqpx, yqq “ σpfpx ¨ yqq “ fpσpx ¨ yqq “ fpσpxq ¨ σpyqq “ ∆pfqpσpxq, σpyqq
so it is a well-defined map of k-algebras, induced by the group law on X. Similarly, we can define
maps ϵ and Σ.
Thus by Remark 22, Spec A is a finite ´etale group scheme over k.

Lemma 2.3.28. Let S be a connected scheme, G Ñ S a finite flat group scheme, and X Ñ S a

torsor over S if and only if there exists a finite locally free surjective morphism Y Ñ S such that

by translations (on the left).

2.3.3
´Etale Fundamental Groups
In previous sections we have illustrated a theory of finite ´etale coverings of schemes and shown that
the classification of those covers is “Galoisienne”. In continuation of a similar analogy with Chapter
1, we now wish to define an object that behaves like the fundamental group but for connected
schemes.
Like in Subsection 1.3, we will define this fundamental group via a fibre functor constructed as
follows:
Construction 2.3.30. Let S be a scheme, and consider the category F´etS. For the rest of the
construction, we fix a geometric point s : Spec Λ Ñ S. We now define a fibre functor on F´etS
as the functor Fibs sending an object X Ñ S in F´etS to the underlying set of its geometric fibre
X ˆS Spec Λ at s. And sends a morphism X Ñ Y from F´etS to the induced set-theoretic map
X ˆS Spec Λ Ñ Y ˆS Spec Λ.
And finally we define an action on the fibres as follow: Consider F : C Ñ D a functor between two
categories. An automorphism of F is a natural transformation ω : F Ñ F that has a two sided
inverse, we write ω P AutpFq. Composition of natural transformations then equips the set AutpFq
with a group structure. Notice that for any object C P C, any ω P AutpFq which is a collection of
bijections ωC : FpCq Ñ FpCq, and given a morphism f : C Ñ C1, we have the following commutative
diagram,

In the literature the geometric fibre of a scheme say X over S at a geometric point say s : Spec Λ Ñ S
is often denoted by Xs :“ X ˆS Spec Λ. In our case however, we want to stress that the main object
of study is actually the functor Fibs and not the geometric point or the scheme. So even though the
commonly used notation would lighten our notations, we stick with writing the fibre functor.
Definition 2.3.31. Given a scheme S and a geometric point s : Spec Λ Ñ S, we define the ´etale
fundamental group πe
1pS, sq as the automorphism group of the fibre functor Fibs on the category
of finite ´etale covers of S.
Remark 25. This obviously implies that πe
1pS, sq acts naturally on FibspXq for any X being a
finite ´etale cover of S, so Fibs takes its values in the category of πe
1pS, sq-sets. However this is not a
monodromy action as it was the case for topological coverings.
In order to understand the ´etale fundamental group of a connected scheme S at a geometric point
s, we need to understand the automorphisms of the fibre functor. And just as it was the case for
topological coverings (a key fact in proving Theorem 1.5.1 and Theorem 1.5.4), a wise step would be
to find a representation of the fibre functor. Awkwardly enough, the functor Fibs is not representable.
Which means there will not be a universal finite ´etale covering. To address this issue, we work in a
more general categorical setting, and introduce the weaker notion of pro-representability. Many of
the following proofs and concepts are inspired by [Luk22].
Definition 2.3.32. Let S be a connected scheme and s a geometric point. We define the cofiltered
category J by
aq Objects of J are pairs pYi, yiq where the Yi P F´etS and yi is an element of FibspYiq for any
Yi P F´etS. We denote objects of J by Yi instead of pYi, yiq when possible
bq Morphisms of J are maps fij : pYj, yjq Ñ pYi, yiq satisfying Fibspfijqpyjq “ yi. And we denote
morphisms in J by the partial order i ď j if such a morphism exist.
Lemma 2.3.33. Let S be a connected scheme and s a geometric point. Let f : X Ñ S be in F´etS
with a fixed geometric point x P FibspXq. Then there exists a unique finite ´etale cover Yk P J that
is Galois over S with a morphism p : Yk Ñ X such that Fibsppqpykq “ x.

Having shown that the ´etale fundamental group is profinite, we will prove that there is again a
classification of finite ´etale covers “`a la Grothendieck”.
Theorem 2.3.39. Let S be a connected scheme, and s : Spec Λ Ñ S a geometric point and
G “ πe
1pS, sq the ´etale fundamental group. The functor Fibs induces an equivalence of category
between F´etS and finite G-sets with a continuous action. Moreover, connected covers correspond to
sets with a transitive G-action, and Galois covers correspond to finite quotients of G.
Proof. Our goal will be to prove that the functor Fibs is fully faithful and essentially surjective
similar to the proof of theorem 1.4.17. We first show fully faithfulness, so let f, g : X Ñ Y be two
morphisms of finite ´etale covers (in F´etS) such that Fibspfq “ Fibspgq. Take i : Z ãÑ X a connected
component, we have Fibspf ˝ iq “ Fibspg ˝ iq and by Proposition 2.3.10, we have that f ˝ i “ g ˝ i.
We do the same for all connected components to obtain that f “ g which shows faithfulness.

There is an interesting way of seeing sets equipped with a certain action that we describe now.
Remark 26. Recall that a permutation representation of a group G on a finite set X is a homo-
morphism ρ : G Ñ SympXq. There is an equivalence of categories between finite sets equipped with
a continuous action of a group G and finite continuous permutation representations of G.
Indeed, in our case, for S a connected scheme and s a geometric point of S, given a continuous action
of πe
1pS, sq onto a finite set X, we define a homomorphism ρ : πe
1pS, sq Ñ SympXq by ρpgqpxq :“ g ¨x.
Then ρ : is a group homomorphism, and it is a continuous map when SympXq is equipped with the
discrete topology. It is thus a finite continuous permutation representation.
On the other hand, given a finite continuous permutation representation ρ : πe
1pS, sq Ñ SympXq,
similarly as above we clearly have that πe
1pS, sq acts continuously on X via the operation defined by
g ¨ x :“ ρpgqpxq, making it a continuous finite πe
1pS, sq-set.
Both constructions being literally the same data viewed differently, composing them gives back ex-
actly what we started with, and the same is true for morphisms of the two categories. Therefore,
we have an equivalence of categories described below.
Corollary 2.3.40. For S a connected scheme with a geometric point s, we have an equivalence of
categories between finite ´etale covers of S and finite continuous permutations representation of its
´etale fundamental group
Our goal was to reconcile the ´etale fundamental group with its topological counterpart. These results
align perfectly with the classical properties of topological fundamental groups, confirming that our
definition of πe
1 is the appropriate algebraic analogue. We can already compute a few examples.
Example 2.3.41. Let S “ Spec k for k a field and s : Spec Λ Ñ S a geometric point with ks Ď Λ.
From Example 2.2.58, connected finite ´etale covers are spectrums of separable field extensions Li
of k. The fibre functor sends Spec Li to the underlying set of Spec pLi bk Λq. The image of the
fibre functor is thus a finite set indexed by the k-algebra homomorphisms L Ñ Λ. Finally, since
the image of each such homomorphism lies in the separable closure ks of k in Λ, we have that
FibspXq “ HomkpL, ksq for all X “ Spec L, so by classical Galois theory, the ´etale fundamental
group is the absolute Galois group πe
1pS, sq “ Galpks|kq.
In particular, πe
1pS, xq is trivial for k
separably closed.
Remark 27. Using the above example, we can justify our initial assumption: that the fibre functor
is not representable. We may think that if Fibs over Spec k were representable then it would be by
Spec ks, although, ks is not a finite ´etale k-algebra. So by Definition B.1.6 even over Spec k the
fibre functor cannot be representable.
Example 2.3.42. Let Fq be a finite field with q elements and denote Fq its closure. From Galois
theory we know that each extension of Fq is of the form Fqn and that they are all Galois with
GalpFqn|Fqq – Z{nZ generated by the Frobenius morphism x ÞÑ xq (see [Lan02], Chapter V. 5).
By the previous example, we know that for fields, πe
1pSpec Fqq is isomorphic to the absolute Galois
group GalpFqq which is itself the profinite completion of Z{nZ by Proposition 1.4.12.
Hence πe
1pSpec Fqq – pZ.
These first two examples already show us that for an algebraic space as “simple” as a point, the
´etale fundamental group, while quite enlightening, can be far from being trivial.
57


## Page 59


2.4
Comparison Theorems
In this section, our goal is to convince the reader that our definition of ´etale fundamental groups
does make sense. We do this by comparing ´etale fundamental group of schemes over C with the
topological fundamental group of complex analytic spaces.
A first issue is that the theorems used to compare such objects are very elaborate. In this thesis,
we present no proof of these renowned theorems, but instead try to motivate and guide their usage.
It is important to know that in the following we will frequently mention concepts or ideas arising
from Riemann surfaces and complex analytic spaces which in themself are already very rich theories
and certainly deserve another presentation like this one. An interesting comment we can already
make about (complex) analytic spaces is that they are topological spaces, which can usually be
embedded with very nice topologies, this is important because it means that our previous discussion
on topological covers is totally applicable here and it will not result in an irrelevant study as it was
the case for schemes.
Before delving into deeper explanations, recall that we have presented in Section C.5 the analytifica-
tion functor, sending objects from the category of algebraic spaces (such as affine varieties, projective
curves or schemes) to objects of the category of analytic spaces (such as manifolds, Riemann surfaces
or complex manifolds).
One of the first comparison theorems available is the following result by Chow from 1949 [Cho18].
Theorem 2.4.1 (Chow). Let χ be a compact analytic subspace of the complex manifold Pn
C. Then
there is a subscheme X Ă Pn with Xan “ χ. Furthermore, holomorphic maps between compact
analytic subspaces of Pn
C induce regular rational morphisms between subschemes.
Proof. See [Cho18] Theorem V .
This theorem is often considered as the first bridge between complex analytical geometry and al-
gebraic geometry. The next collection of results we mention are found in the more than influential
paper written in French by J.P Serre in 1955 called G´eom´etrie Analytique et G´eom´etrie Alg´ebirque
or GAGA for short [Ser56]. Which as the name suggests contains results deepening the connection
between analytical spaces and algebraic ones. In particular, Serre showed that there is an equiva-
lence between the category of coherent sheaves on X and the category of coherent analytic sheaves
on Xan for a X a projective variety over C. From his results, he was able to prove Chow’s theorem
as a consequence of this, GAGA can now provide the backbone for comparing covers of schemes and
covers of analytical spaces. A few of his results are listed in Appendix C.5
For simplicity, from now on we will focus our study on a subcategory of complex analytic spaces,
namely that of compact Riemann surfaces.
2.4.1
Overview of Riemann Surfaces
Definition 2.4.2. A Riemann surfaces X is a 1-dimensional complex manifold. That is, X is
a Hausdorff topological space with an open covering tUi | i P Iu, together with maps fi : Ui Ñ C
called complex charts mapping Ui homeomorphically onto an open subset V Ă C such that for each
pair i, j P I, the map fi ˝ f ´1
j
: fjpUi X Ujq Ñ C is holomorphic. This is called a complex atlas on
X and two complex atlases tUi | i P Iu and tU 1
i | i P Iu on X are equivalent if their union is also a
complex atlas.
From this definition, we can already see that Riemann surfaces inherits the topological property of
locally compactness from C.
Definition 2.4.3. A holomorphic (or analytic) map φ between two Riemann surfaces X and
Y is a continuous map such for each pair of open subsets U Ă X, V Ă Y satisfying fpUq Ă V and
complex charts f : U Ñ C, g : V Ñ C, the functions g ˝ φ ˝ f´1 : gpV q Ñ C are holomorphic.
For more on this topic, we refer to the book Riemann Surfaces [Don11] of S.Donaldson and we
suppose below that basic results of Riemann surfaces and complex analysis are known. We will also
assume that holomorphic maps between Riemann surfaces are non-constant and that the Riemann
surfaces we consider are connected.
58


## Page 60


Remark 28. Since compact Riemann surfaces are obviously topological spaces, one can apply our
theory of topological covering spaces to them. But notice that we obtain much more information on
these covers when they carry the additional structure of a complex manifold. In particular, we can
consider a whole new kind of covers for compact Riemann surfaces.
The following results are well-known facts about holomorphic maps between Riemann surfaces.
Proposition 2.4.4. Let φ : Y Ñ X be a holomorphic map of Riemann surfaces. For each point
y P Y , there is a unique integer k “ ky ě 1 such that we can find complex charts around y in Y and
φpyq in X in which φ is represented by the complex map z ÞÑ zk.
Proof. See Proposition 3 of Chapter 4 in [Don11].
As a natural consequence, one sees that holomorphic maps between Riemann surfaces are open,
indeed the maps z ÞÑ zk is open.
Definition 2.4.5. The above integer ky is called the ramification index of the map φ at y.
And the points y with ky ą 1 are called branch points, we call the set of branch points of φ
ramification locus and denoted it by Bφ
Proposition 2.4.6. Let φ : Y Ñ Y be a holomorphic map of Riemann surfaces. Then the set of
branch points Bφ is finite.
Proof. See Proposition 4 of Chapter 4 from [Don11].
We now restrict our attention to proper maps, recall that a map f : S Ñ T between topological
spaces S, T is said to be proper if for any compact set K Ă T, the preimage f ´1pKq is also compact.
Note that any map between compact topological sets is proper, and for f : S Ñ T a proper map of
topological spaces, its fibre at any point t P T is finite (Proposition 4 Chapter 4 [Don11]).
Lemma 2.4.7. A proper map f : Y Ñ X to a compactly generated Hausdorff space is a closed map
(a space X is called compactly generated if any subset A of X is closed when A X K is closed in K
for each compact K Ă X).
Proof. Let C Ă Y be closed, and let K be a compact subspace of X. Then f ´1pKq is compact
and so is f ´1pKq X C that we denote as B. Then fpBq “ K X fpCq which is compact, indeed
fpC X f ´1pKqq Ď K X fpCq and suppose y P fpCq X K, there exists c P C with y “ fpcq and
for y P K, we have that c P f ´1pKq. And as X is Hausdorff, fpBq is closed. Finally by compact
generation, fpCq is closed in Y .
Notice that locally compact spaces (in particular Riemann surfaces) are compactly generated, thus
we can conclude that the finite set φpBφq of critical values is also closed. We can now state the main
topological property of proper holomorphic maps.
Proposition 2.4.8. Let X be a Riemann surface (connected), and φ : Y Ñ X a proper holomorphic
map. The map φ is surjective with finite fibres, and its restriction to Y z φ´1pφpBφqq is a finite
topological cover.
Proof. Finiteness of fibres follows from the above discussion. And surjectivity follows from the fact
that the image φpY q is open and closed (proper maps are open and closed by Lemma 2.4.7) and that
X is connected. For the last statement, note that by Proposition 2.4.4, each of the finitely many
preimages of x P X zφpBφq has an open neighbourhood mapping homeomorphically onto some open
neighbourhood of x ; the intersection of these is a distinguished open neighbourhood of x as in the
definition of cover.
Definition 2.4.9. We call a proper surjective map of locally compact Hausdorff spaces that restricts
to a cover outside a discrete closed subset a finite branched cover.

We say that a finite branch cover is of degree d or has d many sheets if the covering obtained by
restriction is d-sheeted. And since we have assumed Riemann surfaces to be connected, by Remark
2, the degree of finite branch covers is also constant. In that regard, for X a Riemann surface,
studying covering spaces above XzBφ is done similarly as in Chapter 1. We thus already know how
to compute universal covers, automorphism groups of covers and fundamental groups of Riemann
surfaces.
However, it is interesting to discuss that in the case of compact Riemann surfaces, fundamental
groups are much more restricted. An interesting way to understand this is by looking at the classi-
fication of compact Riemann surfaces,
Theorem 2.4.10. Every compact Riemann surface is homeomorphic to a torus with g holes
Proof. See rDon11s Theorem 3 in Chapter 6.
Remark 29. The takeaway here is that one can construct a torus by identifying opposite sides of
a square, with the same orientation. This generalises for tori with g holes in the following way :
Take a regular 4g-gon and label its sides clockwise by b, a, b, a, d, c, d, c, ..., considering the first
two letters of each quadruplet with clockwise orientation and the last two having counter-clockwise
orientation. By identifying each pair of same letters, respecting the orientation, one obtains a sphere
with g handles, and the sides of each quadruplet get mapped to closed paths all going through a
common point x. The following picture from [Hat02] page 5 is much more intuitive:
The above representation of tori with g holes, together with the classification of compact Riemann
surfaces with g holes allows us to compute the fundamental group of a Riemann surface of genus g.
We conveniently take the base point x to be where paths coming from the first quadruplet a, b, a, b
meet. Then
Theorem 2.4.11. The fundamental group of a compact Riemann surface X of genus g has a finite
presentation of the form π1pX, xq “ xa, b, c, d, ... | ra, bsrc, ds... “ 1y, where the brackets ra, bs denote
the commutators aba´1b´1.
Proof. The proof is an application of Van Kampen’s theorem to cell complexes, see [Hat02] Chapter
1.2
2.4.2
Main Results
Next, we touch upon the main tool for this section, namely, non-singular projective curves over C
and compact Riemann surfaces are equivalent. One direction is easier than the other.
Construction 2.4.12. Let X Ă CPn be a smooth projective curve over C given by r homogeneous
polynomials (with r ě n ´ 1). Consider tUi | i P Iu an affine covering of CPn, then passing to affine
charts, the homogeneous polynomials become holomorphic (polynomial) functions f1, ..., fr : Cn Ñ
C. Recall that since X is a smooth projective variety of dimension 1 (so of codimension n ´ 1), then
60


## Page 62


its Jacobian at any (smooth) point p of X has full rank n ´ 1. So there exists a neighbourhood
V Ă Cn of p P X and n ´ 1 holomorphic functions f1, ..., fn´1 : V Ñ C such that the ideal of X X V
is generated by those n ´ 1 functions. Let us define a map F “ pf1, ..., fn´1q : V Ñ Cn´1. Since
F : Cn Ñ Cn´1 is holomorphic and its differential at p has rank n ´ 1, by the holomorphic implicit
function function theorem, near p, the zero locus F ´1p0q is a complex submanifold of dimension 1.
Thus near every point p P X, the set X is biholomorphic to an open subset of C, giving us local
holomorphic charts on X.
And what is left to check is that these local charts define a global complex structure. Suppose we have
two different systems of equations F “ pf1, ..., fn´1q and G “ pg1, ..., gn´1q as above. By the implicit
function theorem they each give a local parametrisation of X, φ : U Ă C Ñ X, t ÞÑ pt, a2ptq, ..., anptqq
and ψ : V Ă C Ñ X , s ÞÑ ps, b2psq, ..., bnpsqq. It is now easy to check that the transition map
ψ´1 ˝ φ : U Ñ V is holomorphic, because ψ´1 is a biholomorphism onto its image (by the implicit
function theorem) so its inverse is also holomorphic. And on overlaps Ui X Uj, the transition maps
are restrictions of the standard projective coordinate changes Cn Ñ Cn. So X naturally has the
structure of a Riemann surface. Moreover, since X is embedded in a compact space CPn with the
usual topology, X is actually a compact Riemann surface. And we can identify the compact Riemann
surface structure of a smooth projective curve X over C by denoting it Xan.
The other direction is significantly deeper. A proof may require the introduction of notion of divisors
and a thorough study of meromorphic functions, as well as the Riemann-Roch theorem (Theorem
V I, 1.3 in [Har83]) and Chow’s theorem.
A similar reasoning allows us to see smooth projective schemes over C to be compact complex man-
ifolds.
Returning to branched coverings, we now study when finite branched coverings are ´etale. It is im-
portant to note that we are now not only considering finite branched coverings between Riemann
surface, but as morphism of complex analytical spaces, so we also consider the associated structure
sheaf. And the structure sheaf of a Riemann surface X is the sheaf of holomorphic functions, so
for all U Ă X open, OXpUq “ tf : U Ñ C | f holomorphicu. From complex analysis, one knows
that holomorphic functions are locally given by convergent power series, so at any point x P U,
the stalk at x, OX,x consists in germs of functions, where each germ corresponds to a convergent
power series, at each point x, OX,x “ Ctzu. From Proposition 2.4.4, around any point x P X there
exist local coordinates such that a covering f : Y Ñ X is of the form z ÞÑ zk, this is now also
true for the induced map between the structure sheaves. In that regard, on every stalk, we can

Ctzu “ Ctwu ‘ z ¨ Ctwu ‘ ... ‘ zk´1 ¨ Ctwu is a free and finite Ctwu-module of rank k.
So what we have just shown is that finite branch coverings are indeed finite flat morphisms.

Before ending this section, it is worthwhile pointing out that the equivalence of categories between
compact Riemann surfaces and projective curves over C is deeper. Indeed, we have the following
three-way equivalence of categories:
aq Smooth projective algebraic curves over C and non-constant algebraic maps,
bq Compact connected Riemann surfaces and non-constant holomorphic maps, and
cq Finite field extensions of C of transcendence degree 1 and morphisms of extensions of C.
with the first two categories being anti-equivalent to the third one. This situation is explained in
detail in Chapter 4 of [Sza09].

2.5
Properties of ´Etale Fundamental Groups
In this section, we discuss a few interesting properties of the ´etale fundamental group. Namely, we
prove its insensitivity to the change of geometric base points for connected schemes; this should not
be surprising as it was already the case for the topological fundamental group. Then, as the ´etale
fundamental group is an object emanating from algebraic geometry, we will study how it behaves
under functorial base change. And finally, we finish this section by providing useful tools for com-
puting ´etale fundamental groups, relying on powerful exact sequences.
Let us first show the following lemma.

s1 : Spec Λ1 Ñ S, there exists an isomorphism of fibre functors Fibs

Proposition 2.5.12. Let L be an extension of an algebraically closed field k and X be a proper
integral scheme over k.

Proof. If Y Ñ X is a connected finite ´etale cover, then Y is reduced by Proposition 2.3.7 since X is
also reduced (Proposition C.2.9). Since Y is reduced, so is the generic fibre Yη by Proposition 2.3.7.
And by Lemma 1.4.20, Yη is the spectrum of a direct sum of field extensions of KpXq, the function
field of X. The generic fibre also being connected, it must be a single finite field extension of the
function field of X. Now Y is also irreducible, it has 1 minimal prime ideal (as does Yη), then Y is
an integral scheme as well. And since k is algebraically closed in L, the tensor product KpY q bk L
is a field, where KpY q is the function field of Y . This shows that the base change SpecpKpY q bk Lq,
the part of YL lying over its generic point η, is connected, and in turn, that YL is connected. With
Proposition 2.5.4 bq, we have that πe
1pXL, xLq Ñ πe
1pX, xq is surjective.
For injectivity, this is where the previous corollary intervenes. We refer to Corollaire X. 1.8 from
[Gro03] or Proposition 5.6.7 in [Sza09].

Q ãÑ C and get that the ´etale fundamental group of X over Q is topologically finitely generated at
every geometric point. And by considering the inclusion Q ãÑ k, for k an algebraically closed field
of characteristic 0 we get the following result.
Corollary 2.5.13. Let k be an algebraically closed field of characteristic 0, and let X be a smooth
connected projective scheme over k. For every geometric base point x the ´etale fundamental group
πe
1pX, xq is topologically finitely generated.

68


## Page 70


Grothendieck suggested that the most interesting group in mathematics is arguably πe
1pP1
Qzt0, 1, 8u, xq.
Later on, he advised P. Deligne to study this group, which he did extensively, leading to a 200-pages
monograph [Del89]. In his paper, Deligne introduced a Tannakian framework for the category of
mixed motives. Without delving further into motivic theory, we can summarize Deligne’s objective
in defining a “motivic” version of the fundamental group through the following question:
There are many ways through which one can understand a (algebraic) space, this can be done through
theories such as Betti cohomology, de Rham cohomology, ℓ-adic cohomology and crystalline coho-
mology. The problem is that these theories may feel like different “shadows” of the same object.
Now Grothendieck hoped for a universal theory, sitting above them all, which he called the theory
of motives. And Deligne’s approach consisted of finding a way to unify this perspective by applying
it to objects richer than cohomology groups: non-abelian fundamental groups.
In [Del89], he successfully defined what it meant for a non-abelian group to be motivic by working
with a Tannakian realisation of the ´etale fundamental group of P1
Qzt0, 1, 8u as a non-trivial example.
This the motivation behind the next chapter.
69


## Page 71


3
Tannakian Fundamental Groups
In the last chapter, we established an equivalence of categories between the category of finite ´etale
covers over a connected scheme and that of finite continuous representations of its ´etale fundamental
group. In this chapter, we study a linearisation of this concept by replacing finite ´etale covers by
linear algebraic data. We begin by studying representations of affine group schemes and their relation
with comodules over Hopf algebras. Then introduce monoidal categories and develop the categorical
language required for Tannaka duality. And finish with a specialisation of this reconstruction for
neutral Tannakian categories, and define their Tannakian fundamental group.
3.1
Representation of Group Schemes and Comodules
Before defining comodules, one has to make sense of the kind of object we define comodules over. It
is quite natural that we want comodules to be the dual notion of modules. And since we will study
modules over algebras, we introduce below the notion of a coalgebra. This will allow us to have a
clean definition for coations of comodules.
Definition 3.1.1. A coalgebra is a k-vector space C endowed with a k-linear comultiplication :
∆: C Ñ C bk C and a k-linear counit : ϵ : C Ñ k satisfying the following coassociativity and
counity axioms,

One can see from the above definition that coalgebras over k are the dual notion of algebras over k.
The next result tells us that under a finiteness condition, dualising a coalgebra, we get an algebra
as well.
Proposition 3.1.2. The contravariant dualisation functor A ÞÑ A˚ induces an anti-equivalence
between the category of finite-dimensional k-coalgebras and that of finite-dimensional k-algebras.
Proof. Given a k-coalgebra A, the k-linear dual A˚ “ HomkpA, kq of the underlying finite-dimensional
k-vector space carries additional structure. The comultiplication ∆induces a k-bilinear map m :
A˚bk A˚ Ñ A˚ mapping a pair pf, gq of maps in HomkpA, kq to the k-linear map pf bgq˝∆: A Ñ k.
The counit ϵ has a k-linear dual e : k Ñ A˚ determined by the image of 1 P k in A˚. By the coas-
sociativity and counity axioms, the maps m and e define a multiplication and a unit satisfying
associativity and unity, making A˚ a k-algebra.
Conversely, given a finite-dimensional k-algebra B, the multiplication map m induces a map B˚ Ñ
pB b Bq˚ on the k-linear duals. Here enters our crucial assumption that algebras must be finite-
dimensional. Indeed, the natural map B˚ b B˚ Ñ pB b Bq˚ sending pf, gq to the k-linear map
a b b ÞÑ fpaqgpbqq is an isomorphism if B is finite-dimensional over k, in that case it is an injective
map between vector spaces of the same dimension. And by reversing the above procedure, we can
endow B˚ with a structure of k-coalgebra.
Remark 33. The above equivalence restricts to the finite-dimensional case, because whereas it is
true that arbitrary k-coalgebras dualise into algebras, the converse is false for infinite-dimensional
k-algebras. For example, the k-algebra A “ krxs does not dualise into a coalgebra. Denote A˚ “
pkrxsq˚, then the natural map A˚ b A˚ Ñ pA b Aq˚ is not surjective anymore. Indeed, elements
of A˚ b A˚ are finite sum tensors (they have finite rank) while elements of pA b Aq˚ are infinite
sequences of evaluation on the basis.
Definition 3.1.3. A bialgebra H is a vector space that is at the same time an algebra with
multiplication m and unit u and a coalgebra with comultiplication ∆and counit ϵ, such that ∆and
ϵ are algebra morphisms (or equivalently that m and u are coalgebra morphisms).
A Hopf algebra is a bialgebra that possesses an additional map Σ : H Ñ H, which makes the

Theorem 3.1.5. Let C be a k-coalgebra and M a right C-comodule. The any element m P M is

Remark 35. Recall that in the last chapter, we identified group schemes with group-valued repre-
sentable functors (Proposition 2.3.23). And that in particular, we can see affine group schemes G
over k as representable group-valued functors R ÞÑ HomkpSpec R, Gq from the category of k-algebras
to that of groups, that is represented by the coordinate ring A of G which is a Hopf algebra (Remark
22). In that case, a representation pV, ρq of an affine group scheme G consists of giving for each
k-algebra R a GpRq Ñ EndpV bkRq. Functorially, this amounts to defining a natural transformation
ρ : G Ñ GL.
Proposition 3.1.6. There is a bijection between right comodules over the commutative Hopf algebra
A and left representations of the corresponding affine group scheme.
Proof. Let M be an A-comodule where A is a commutative Hopf algebra. Given a k-algebra R,
an element of GpRq corresponds to a k-algebra homomorphism g : A Ñ R. The composite map
πR : M

On the other hand, let pV, ρq be a left representation of an affine group scheme G, so it has an

The composition of πApidAq with v ÞÑ v b 1 defines a map ν : V Ñ V b A, which can be seen as
a coaction on the A-comodule V . The two construction being inverse to one another, we have a
bijection.

## Page 73


What we have seen so far is that for an affine group scheme G, one can consider its coordinate
ring, which is a Hopf algebra. Then studying its category of representations is the same as studying
comodules over its coordinate ring. Now we want to know if the reverse computation is possible. By
that, we mean if it is possible to recover the affine group scheme G from its category of representations
(which is equivalent to a certain category of comodules). This problem has been extensively studied
and is largely understood through the lens of Tannaka-Krein duality. We shall give a somewhat
motivation for this.
We start with the theory of Morita equivalence, which states that one can
determine an algebra A from its category of modules ModA up to Morita equivalence (see [EGNO15],
Chapter 7.8). But in the case of finite-dimensional, by considering the associated forgetful functor
ω : ModA Ñ Vectk, our k-algebra can be recovered up to isomorphism as the endomorphism
algebra of the forgetful functor. If A is a finite-dimensional k-algebra, then the category of finite-
dimensional A-modules is a k-linear abelian category. The other way around, for C a k-linear abelian
category, then one can take A “ EndpPqop for P a progenerator of C. That same progenerator in C
represents a forgetful functor ω : C Ñ Vectf.d.
k , giving us A “ EndpPqop – Endpωq (see [EGNO15],
Chapter 1.8). And a similar statement holds for arbitrary k-algebras. What we are interested in is
what happens in the case of comodules (corresponding to representations), we want to dualise the
finite-dimensional statement to obtain a similar reconstruction but for coalgebras from a category of
comodules over a coalgebra. However, as we saw in Remark 33 a similar procedure in the infinite-
dimensional case fails in general. Still, it is possible to recover an arbitrary k-coalgebra C from the
category CoModf.d.

of finite-dimensional right C-comodules with the associated forgetful functor

Tannaka-Krein duality tells us that the coalgebra we recover from its category of finite-dimensional

tions. We now introduce the language necessary to understand those extra conditions.
3.2
Monoidal Categories and Tannaka-Krein Theorem
Monoidal categories, also often called tensor categories, can be seen as the generalisation of categories
carrying a tensor product, such as the categories of vector spaces, abelian groups, R-modules, or
R-algebras. We will use the theory developed in the previous section to prove that the category
of finite-dimensional representations of an affine group scheme over k is equivalent to a monoidal
category under necessary and sufficient conditions. And we will end this section by introducing the
Tannaka-Krein Duality theorem.
3.2.1
Monoidal Categories
Definition 3.2.1. A monoidal category is a category C together with a bifunctor b : C ˆC Ñ C
called the tensor product, a unit object 1 in C together with natural isomorphisms Φ of functors
from C ˆ C ˆ C to C given on a triple pX, Y, Zq of objects by

The first diagram is called the associativity constraint, while the second is referred to as the unitary

Remark 38. A consequence of Mac Lane’s coherence theorem is that everything that can be proven
in a strict monoidal category is also valid in a monoidal category. For that reason, we will usually
denote monoidal categories by tuples pC, b, Iq, omitting associativity and unitary constraints. We
will formulate this coherence theorem later but assume its validity from now on.

We define symmetrically right dual objects in a monoidal category and say that a monoidal category
is right dual if all of its objects admit right duals. If every object of a monoidal category admits
simultaneously right and left duals, we say that the category is rigid. Notice that if a monoidal
category is symmetric, it is left rigid if and only if it is right rigid and its left and right duals coincide.

Remark 40. If a monoidal functor is an equivalence of categories, then it is automatically a strong
monoidal functor
The next theorem is Mac Lane’s coherence theorem. It can be understood as a strictification result
and the original statement along with its original proof can be found in [ML98], Chapter V II. 2 of
Theorem 3.2.9. Any monoidal category is monoidally equivalent to a strict monoidal category.
Some consequences of Mac Lane’s coherence theorem are the following proposition.
Proposition 3.2.10. Strong monoidal functors between rigid monoidal categories preserve duals.

3.2.2
Tannaka-Krein Duality
Before finishing this section, we have to talk about the Tannaka-Krein Duality. This result can be
understood as a reconstruction result. Usually, its statement holds for any k-linear abelian category
78


## Page 80


(see Remark 37), and it automatically gives that CoModf.d. is a k-linear abelian category (which
we have already reviewed, see B.4.4). Thus, below we state a restriction of the famous Tannaka
Duality.
Theorem 3.2.14 (Tannaka Duality). Let C be a k-coalgebra for k a field and ω : CoModf.d.
C

algebra.
cq CoModf.d.

is a rigid symmetric monoidal category and ω is a symmetric monoidal functor if
and only if C has the structure of a commutative Hopf algebra.

3.3
Neutral Tannakian Categories and Tannakian Fundamental Groups
We are finally able to introduce Tannakian categories and the Tannakian fundamental group.
Definition 3.3.1. A neutral Tannakian category over a field k is a rigid, k-linear, abelian,
symmetric monoidal category C whose unit 1 satisfies Endp1q – k, and is moreover equipped with
an exact faithful tensor functor ω : C Ñ Vectf.d.
k
. The functor ω is called a (neutral) fibre functor
Example 3.3.2. Let G be an affine group scheme over k.
The category Repf.d.pGq of finite-
dimensional representations of G with its usual tensor product and the forgetful functor ω : Repf.d.pGq Ñ
Vectf.d.
k
as the fibre functor is a neutral Tannkian category.
The converse is a consequence of Tannaka duality 3.2.14 and we get the following theorem,
Theorem 3.3.3. Neutral Tannakian categories over k are equivalent to categories of finite-dimensional
representations of an affine group schemes over k.
Proof. This is a direct application of Theorem 3.2.14 and Proposition 3.1.6.
Definition 3.3.4. Consider pC, ωq a Tannakian category. The associated functor Autbpωq is called
the Tannakian fundamental group of pC, ωq
Notice that with the previous theorem and Proposition 3.2.13, the Tannakian fundamental group is
always isomorphic to some group scheme as a functor.
Our next goal is to give a result identifying somewhat quotients of Tannakian fundamental groups
with affine subgroup schemes of GLn. But first, let us give two constructions:
Construction 3.3.5. Let G be an arbitrary group and k a field.
Then the category of finite-
dimensional representations of such an arbitrary group is still a neutral Tannakian with its usual
forgetful functor. The Tannakian fundamental group of its category of finite-dimensional represen-
tations together with the forgetful functor is usually called the algebraic hull . One can also consider
the algebraic hull of a topological group by taking its category of finite-dimensional continuous
representations.
79


## Page 81


The next construction tells us how to consider quotient of the Tannakian fundamental group,
Construction 3.3.6. Given a Tannakian category pC, ωq and an object X P C, we denote xXyb the
smallest full Tannakian subcategory of C containing X. Its objects are subquotients of finite direct
sums of objects of the form Xbr b pX˚qbs for some r, s ě 0.
And we have the following result,
Proposition 3.3.7. Let G be an arbitrary group, k a field and pV, ρq finite-dimensional represen-
tation of G over k. Then the Tannakian fundamental group of the full Tannakian subcategory xV yb
of Repf.d.
k
pGq is canonically isomorphic to the Zariski closure of the image of ρ in GLn.
Notice that im ρ is actually an affine group scheme in GLn. It is a closed subvariety and since G is
a group, using the fact ρpGq Ă ρpGq, then ρpGq has a group structure.
In order to prove the above proposition, we need a lemma that is equivalent to the following Theorem
in [Wat79]
Theorem 3.3.8 (Waterhouse). Let k be a field and G a closed subgroup scheme of GLn. Every
finite-dimensional representation of G can be reconstructed from its original representation on kn by
the process of forming tensor products, direct sums, subrepresentations, quotients and duals.
Proof. See [Wat79] Chapter 3.5.
Corollary 3.3.9. Let k be a field and G a closed subgroup scheme of GLn over k. Denote by X the
n-dimensional representation of G corresponding to its action on kn via the inclusion G Ñ GLn.
Then the full Tannakian subcategory xXyb Ă RepkpGq is the whole of RepkpGq.
Proof. Since Tannakian categories are rigid monoidal and abelian, they are closed under taking the
tensor product, dual, direct sum and taking quotients and subrepresentations since both last are
instance of kernels and cokernels.
Proof of Proposition 3.3.7. Denote G the affine group scheme over k defined by im ρ in GLn. The
representation ρ : V Ñ GLnpV q give rise to a representation of the affine group scheme G in a
natural way by taking the inclusion G Ñ GLnpV q, we denote it by V . Similarly, any object of xV yb
give rise to an object of RepG and e obtain an equivalence between xV yb and the full Tannakian
subcategory xV yb of RepG. In particular the two categories have isomorphic Tanakian fundamental
group. But by the above corollary, xV yb is the whole of RepG, proving our proposition.
Example 3.3.10. One interesting example of Tannakian categories, is that of complex local sys-
tems.
Consider X a connected and locally simply-connected topological space and fix x P X
a base point.
The category LSX of local systems on X is a C-linear abelian category (since
LCSCpXq » ModCrπ1pX,xqs, and a category of R-modules is abelian). With the usual tensor prod-
uct and dual construction from linear algebra, LSX is endowed with a symmetric rigid monoidal
structure. Taking the stalk of a local system at x yields a fibre functor with values in Vectf.d.
C . This
means that LSX with the functor taking stalks of local systems at x is a neutral Tannakian category.
Recall that in Corollary 1.6.12 we have established an equivalence of categories between complex
local systems on X and finite-dimensional representations of the fundamental group π1pX, xq. No-
tice that this is an equivalence of (neutral) Tannakian categories, thus the Tannakian fundamental
group of LSX is isomorphic to the algebraic hull of π1pX, xq over C as in Construction 3.3.5.
Finally, using our previous Proposition 3.3.7, the Tannakian fundamental group of each full Tan-
nakian subcategory xSyb of LSX is isomorphic to the Zariski closure of the image of ρS,x : π1pX, xq Ñ
GLnpCq.
3.3.1
Nori’s Fundamental Group Scheme
We end this chapter with a correspondence between the above Tannakian formalism and the ´etale
fundamental group of schemes from the previous chapter presented by Nori in his PhD thesis [Nor82].
For S a proper integral scheme over a field k and s : Spec k Ñ S a k-rational point, Nori showed
that the full subcategory of essentially finite vector bundles over S denoted EFS of the category

of locally free sheaves on S spanned by the essentially finite sheaves together with the usual tensor
product of sheaves and the functor E Ñ s˚E, is a neutral Tannakian category over k (see section

Definition 3.3.11. For S and s as above, we define the Nori fundamental group scheme of
S at the base point s to be the Tannakian fundamental group of the neutral Tannakian category
pEFS, s˚q mentionned above. We denote it by πN
1 pS, sq.
Proposition 3.3.12. The fundamental group scheme πN
1 pS, sq is an inverse limit of finite k-group
schemes.
Proof. See [Nor82], Conclusion I. 3.
The majority of [Nor82] focuses on what happens to that fundamental group, in the case where S
is defined over fields of positive characteristic. However, what is really surprising is that over a field
of characteristic 0, we can compare the Nori fundamental group scheme and the ´etale fundamental
group scheme. Indeed, this is due to the fact that in characteristic 0, every finite group scheme is
´etale and combining this with the above Proposition 3.3.12, gives us the following corollary.
Corollary 3.3.13. When k is an algebraically closed field of characteristic 0.

Selected Results from Commutative Algebra and Groups
Theory
For this thesis, most results from (non-)commutative algebra that can be found in [Lan02] and
[Eis95] are assumed to be known by the reader. However, for the sake of keeping this document
self-contained, we recall a few important and necessary results.
Theorem A.0.1. Let Abe a subring of B, let p be a prime ideal in A and assume that B is integral
over A. Then pB ‰ B and there exists a prime ideal q in B lying above p
Proof. See Proposition 1.10 in Chapter V II, §1 of [Lan02].
The following lemmas are the famous Nakayama Lemmas. They have many different statements,
we give two of them here and the latter can be seen as a consequence of the former.
Lemma A.0.2 (Nakayama). Let I be an ideal in R, and M a finitely generated module over R. If
IM “ M, then there exists r P R with r ” 1 mod I such that rM “ 0.
Lemma A.0.3 (Nakayama). Let I be an ideal in R, and M a finitely generated R-module.
If
I “ IM, then there exists an i P I such that im “ m for all m P M.
A.1
Krull Dimension and Artinian Rings
Definition A.1.1. The Krull dimension of a commutative ring R is the supremum of the lengths
of all increasing chain of prime ideals in R
Definition A.1.2. A ring R is called Noetherian if there is no infinite increasing sequence of ideals
I1 Ĺ I2 Ĺ ¨ ¨ ¨ Ĺ In Ĺ . . . , while it is called Artinian if there is no infinite descending sequence of
ideals I1 Ľ I2 Ľ ¨ ¨ ¨ Ľ In Ľ . . . .
Example A.1.3.
aq Let k be a field, then finite-dimensional k-algebras are Artinian.
bq The ring of polynomials with finite variables over a field, such as krx1, . . . , xns is Noetherian.
In the following we wish to show that ant Artinian ring has zero Krull dimension.
Lemma A.1.4. Let A be an Artinian ring which is also an integral domain. Then A is a field.
Proof. Let x P A be non-zero. We hae a descending chain 1 Ě pxq Ě px2q..., which must stabilise,
so pxnq “ pxn`1q for some n. This implies that xn “ yxn`1 for some y P A. Since A is an integral
domain and x ‰ 0, we can cancel xn to get xy “ 1 making x a unit. This holds for all non-zero
x P A, hence the ring A is a field.
Lemma A.1.5. In an Artinian ring, every prime ideal is maximal.
Proof. If A is Artinian and p Ă A is a prime ideal, then A{p is still Artinian. Since A{p is an integral
domain, by the previous lemma it is a field, so p is actually a maximal ideal.
Proposition A.1.6. A ring A has Krull dimension zero if and only if every prime ideal is maximal.
Proof. If p Ă A is a non-maximal prime ideal, then there is some maximal ideal m with p Ĺ m,
giving a chain of length 1, and so dim A ě 1. Conversely, if dim A ě 1, then there is some chain of
prime ideals p0 Ľ p1 Ľ ..., whereby p1 is not a maximal prime ideal.
Corollary A.1.7. Artinian rings have zero Krull dimension.

Classification of Transitive G-Spaces
The result we mention in this subsection is used extensively in the first chapter. It is quite powerful
as it gives a classification of transitive G-spaces by means of stabiliser.
Theorem A.2.1.
aq Let Ωbe a transitive G-space. Then Ωis isomorphic to the coset space G{H where H “ Gα,
the stabiliser for α P Ω.
bq Two coset spaces G{H and G{K are isomorphic if and only if H and K are conjugate subgroups
of G.
Proof. See Theorem 1.3 of [Cam99].

- faithful if and only if FXY is injective for all objects X, Y P C,
- full if and only if FXY is surjective for all objects X, Y P C,
- fully faithful if and only if FXY is bijective for all objects X, Y P C,

Clearly, a subcategory D of C is itself a category and there is a faithful functor D Ñ C. A subcategory
is said to be full if this functor is moreover a full functor.

The following well-known lemma is of pivotal importance. Observe that if X and Y are objects of C,

Limits
Definition B.2.1. Let F : Z Ñ C be a covariant functor. A cone on F is couple pM, µq, where

An important construction we can make from filtered limits are pro-objects. They allow one to work
with systems that behave like a limit even if a limit does not exist in a category. We enumerate the
following interesting pro-objects :
Definition B.2.8. A profinite group is defined to be an inverse limit of a system of finite groups.
Definition B.2.9. Let C be a category, and F a set-valued functor on C. We say that F is pro-
representable if there exists an direct system pCi, fijq of objects and morphisms in C indexed by
the directed set pI, ďq and a functorial isomorphism lim
ÝÑ HompCi, Xq – FpXq for each object X P C.
A way of generalising the notion of directed system is by considering the directed system to be a
category called (co)-filtered in the following way :
Definition B.2.10. A category J is said to be filtered when it is non-empty, for every two objects
J and J1 in J , there exists an object K and two morphisms f : J Ñ K and f1 : J1 Ñ K in J , and
for every two morphisms u, v : I Ñ J in J , there exists an object K and a morphism w : J Ñ K
such that w ˝ u “ w ˝ v. Dually, a category J is said to be cofiltered if the opposite category J op
is filtered.
The category Z in Lemma B.2.4 and Definition B.2.5 is first a filtered category and secondly a
cofiltered category and the functor F : Z Ñ C is thus often called a filtered diagram and dually a
cofiltered diagram.
Theorem B.2.11. Let K be a subcatecory of a filtered category J such that it is a cofinal set.
Suppose that the functors F : J Ñ C and F 1 : K Ñ C have colimits. Then there is a canonical
isomorphism between there colimits.
Proof. See [ML98], Theorem IX.3.1
A subset I Ă J of a poset J is said to be cofinal if for every j P J, there exists some i P I such that
j ď i.

Whether a product or a coproduct exists may depend on C or on X1 and X2, however if it does
exists it is unique up to canonical isomorphism.
In a category C with finite product an object X is said to be a group object if it has as morphisms,
a group multiplication and inverse operation, as well as an identity such that the multiplication is
associative and the inverse and the identity are two-sided.
Kernels and Cokernels
Let C be a category with f : X Ñ Y a morphism in C.
An equaliser consists of an object E P C and a morphism eq : E Ñ X in C such that for two parallel
morphisms f, g : X Ñ Y and for any morphism m : Z Ñ X in C, there exists a unique morphism
u : Z Ñ E such that the following diagram commutes
