In that casen pE, eqq is said to be an equaliser for f and g. Similarly we define a coequaliser pQ, qq
in C as the dual of the equaliser.
A kernel of a morphism f : X Ñ Y in C is an equaliser of f and the zero morphism from X to Y .
A cokernel of f in C is defined as the coequaliser of f and the zero morphism from X to Y .
Initial and terminal objects:
Proposition B.2.12. Let Z “ H be the empty category, and let F : Z Ñ C be the unique functor.
Then
iq the limit of F is an object T in C with the property that for any (other) object C P C there
exists a unique morphism C Ñ T. In particular, HompT, Tq is a singleton. If T exists, it is
called a (the) terminal object of C.
iiq the colimit of F is an object I P C with the property that for any object C P C, there exists
a unique morphism I Ñ C. In particular, HompI, Iq is a singleton. If I exists, it is called a
(the) initial object of C.
Remark that if they exist, the initial and terminal object are unique up to isomorphism.

Adjoint Functors
Definition B.3.1. Let C and D be categories; let L : C Ñ D and R : D Ñ C be functors. We say

L if and only if for any objects C P C and D P D, there is an isomorphism
θC,D : HomDpLC, Dq Ñ HomCpC, RDq
that is natural in both arguments C and D. If there exists an adjoint pair of functors between the
categories C and D, we refer to this situation as an adjunction and denote it by pL, Rq : C Õ D

right S-module M a right R-module structure by the formula m ¨ r “ m ¨ ϕprq, m P M, r P R.
This functor has a left adjoint, called the extension of scalars, given by ´bR S : MR Ñ MS.
bq The pull-back and pushout functors defined above are respectively left and right adjoint one
to another.

B.4
Abelian Categories
Definition B.4.1. A category is preadditive if every HomC-sets are abelian groups and the com-
position of morphisms is bilinear.
A preadditive category is additive if every finite set of objects has a biproduct, so we can form firect
sums and direct products (which are isomorphic) and zero object (which are empty biproducts).
An additive category is preabelian if every morphism has both a kernel and a cokerel.
Finally, a preablian category is abelian if every monomorphism is a kernel of some morphism and
every epimorphism is a cokernel of some morphism.
An additive category k-linear if every of its Hom-sets are k-vector space.
Definition B.4.2. A functor F : C Ñ D between two preadditive categories is called additive if
Fpf ` gq “ Fpfq ` Fpgq for any two object A, B P C and any two morphisms f, g P HomCpA, Bq.
A functor F : C Ñ D between two preabelian categories is called exact if for any exact sequence of the

aq Trivially, the category of abelian groups is an abelian category.
bq Let R be a ring, then the category of R-modules is an abelian category.

C-comodules is k-linear and abelian.

B.5
Equivalence of Categories
We have introduced the notion of an isomorphism of categories (a pair of functors F : C Ñ D, G :
D Ñ C such that F ˝ G “ 1D and G ˝ F “ 1C). This definition is however very rigid and many
natural examples of “equivalent theories” do not fall under this definition.
Hence, it would be very reasonable to introduce a new definition where we replace the above identities
by natural isomorphisms
Definition B.5.1. Two categories C and D are equivalent if there exist two functors F : C Ñ D
and G : D Ñ C, and two natural isomorphisms α : F ˝ G
„
ÝÑ 1D and β : G ˝ F
„
ÝÑ 1C. In this
situation we say that the functor G is a quasi-inverse for F (and F is a quasi-inverse for G). Finally,
we say that C and D are anti-equivalent if C is equivalent to Dop.

ε are natural isomorphisms.

aq F is an equivalence of categories,
bq F is part of an adjoint equivalence C Õ D.
cq F is fully faithful and essentially surjective
Proof. See [ML98], Theorem IV. 4.1.
B.6
Grothendieck Topologies
We finish our review on categories by an interesting way of endowing a category with a sort of
topology called a Grothendieck topology. These kinds of topologies allow to axiomatise the notion
of an open cover for a category.
Definition B.6.1. A Grothendieck topology G on a category C is a collection of maps GpXq “
tpUi Ñ XqiPIu for all X P C called coverings, such that,
• If X1 Ñ X is an isomorphism, then tX1 Ñ Xu P GpXq.
• If tUi Ñ XuiPI P GpXq and W Ñ X, then tUi ˆX W Ñ Wu P GpXq.
• If tUi Ñ XuiPI P GpXq and tVij Ñ UiujPJ P GpUiq for all i P I, then tVij Ñ Xupi,jqPIˆJ P
GpXq.
We can now define the analogue of topological spaces but for categories,
Definition B.6.2. A site is a pair pC, Gq, such that C is a category and G is a Grothendieck topology
on C.
Now it becomes possible to define sheaves on a category and so it is even possible to define their
sheaf cohomology.
Remark 43. As it is the case for topological spaces, categories can be endowed with many different
Grothendieck topologies. The category of schemes is of particular interest to us here.
The category Sch can be endowed with the elementary Zariski topology, in that case a Zariski cov-
ering is a family of morphisms tpfi : Ui Ñ XqiPIu of schemes such that each fi is an open immersion
and such that X “ Ť
iPI fipUiq.
We gave an introduction to ´etale topology on the category of schemes in Chapter 2.1.
And as other classes of Grothendieck topologies we may find flat topologies. Among them we have
the fppf topology (as in fid`element plate de pr´esentation finie) and the fpqc topology (as in fid`element
plate et quasi-compacte). In these topologies, the morphisms of affine schemes that are covering
morphisms are respectively faithfully flat, of finite presentation, and is quasi-finite, and faithfully
flat and quasi-compact.
C
Review of Algebraic Geometry
Often, before introducing schemes, one defines the notion of an algebraic variety. It can be seen as
the set of common solutions of a system of polynomial equations over a field (usually algebraically
closed). Here, we take the liberty of referring to the first chapter of [Har83], and immediately start
by introducing the theory of sheaves, then schemes.
89


## Page 91


C.1
Sheaves
Definition C.1.1. A presheaf F of sets presp. rings, abelian groups, R-modules, ...q on a topo-
logical space X consists of the following data:
aq for every open set U Ă X, a set presp. ring, abelian group, R-module, ...q FpUq, and
bq for every inclusion V Ă U of open sets in X a morphism of sets presp. rings, abelian groups,
R-modules, ...q ρUV : FpUq Ñ FpV q called the restriction map, such that
1q FpHq “ 0,
2q ρUU is the identity map on FpUq for all open sets U Ă X, and
3q for any inclusion W Ă V Ă U of opens of X, we have ρUV ˝ ρV W “ ρUW .
The elements of FpUq are called sections of F over U.
We can reformulate the definition of a presheaf in the language of category, for any topological space
X, we define a category ToppXq whose objects are open subsets of X and where the morphisms
are inclusion maps. So if V Ă U in X, HompU, V q “ H and HompV, Uq has just one element. Now
a presheaf is just a contravariant functor from the category ToppXq to the category of sets (resp.
rings, abelian groups, R-modules, ...).
Definition C.1.2. Now a presheaf F is a called a sheaf if it satisfies the following glueing property
:
For U Ă X a non-empty open subset, tUi : i P Iu a non-empty open cover of U and si P FpUiq such
that si|UiXUj “ sj|UiXUj for all i, j P I, then there is a unique s P FpUq such that s|Ui “ si for all
i P I.
Definition C.1.3. Let F be a presheaf on X, and let U Ă X be an open subset. Then the restriction
of F to U is defined to be the presheaf F|U on U with F|UpV q “ FpV q for every open subset V Ă U,
and with the restriction maps taken from F . Note that if F is a sheaf then so is F|U
Examples C.1.4.
aq Let X be a complex manifold. Due to the maximum modulus principle from complex analysis,
we know that the only holomorphic functions f : X Ñ C are the constant functions.
To
resolve this challenge, we work “locally” and define the following sheaf OX to keep track of
the holomorphic structure on the underlying topological space of X on arbitrary open subsets.
For every open subset U Ă X, we define OXpUq to be the ring (commutative) of holomorphic
functions from U to C with the restriction being the restriction of domain of functions.
bq Let S be a topological space (or abelian group, ...), then we can define a sheaf FS on any
topological space X by setting FSpUq to be the set (or abelian group, ...) of continuous maps
U Ñ S and taking the restriction maps to be the usual restriction of functions. Accordingly,
this construction defines a sheaf. Moreover, if S has the discrete topology, every continuous
map f : U Ñ S is constant on each connected component of U, so FSpUq “ S. And in that
case, we call FS the constant sheaf on X with value S.
Definition C.1.5. If F and G are presheaves on X, a morphism of presheaves φ : F Ñ G
consists of a morphism of sets (resp. rings, abelian groups, R-modules, ...) φU : FpUq Ñ GpUq for
each open set U, such that for every inclusion V Ă U, the following diagram commutes,

This forms a category of presheaves of sets (resp. rings, abelian groups, R-modules, ...) on a fixed
topological space X, denoted PShpXq. And the category of sheaves of sets (resp. rings, abelian
groups, R-modules, ...) on X, denoted ShpXq is defined as the full subcategory of the corresponding
presheaf category. This means that a morphism of sheaves is just a morphism of the underlying
presheaves.

The process of considering a sheaf associated to a presheaf is called sheafification and is described
in [Har83] Proposition-Definition II. 1.2.
C.2
Schemes
In classical algebraic geometry, a scheme is a generalisation of a variety (it allows to consider non-
reduced rings and thus it allows for nilpotents). We begin with the affine case.
Definition C.2.1. Let R be a ring. The set of all prime ideals of R is called the spectrum of R
or the affine scheme associated to R, and is denoted by Spec R.
Definition C.2.2. Let R be a ring and P P Spec R a prime ideal of R (a point in the corresponding
affine scheme).
aq We denote κpPq the quotient field of the integral domain R{P. It is called the residue field
of Spec R at P.
bq For any f P R, we define the value of f at P, to be the image of f under the composite ring
homomorphism R Ñ R{P Ñ κpPq.
Definition C.2.3. An affine scheme Spec R is called integral if it is non-empty and the ring R is
an integral domain.
Definition C.2.4. An affine scheme Spec R is called reduced if the ring R is also reduced, i.e. it
contains no nilpotent elements.
Let R be a ring, similarly as in the case of affine varieties, we define the zero locus V pSq of S Ă R
and the ideal IpXq of X Ă Spec R to be :
V pSq :“ tP P Spec R | fpPq “ 0 , @ f P Su Ă Spec R,
IpXq :“ tf P R | fpPq “ 0, @ P P Xu Ĳ R
We can now endow an affine scheme with a topology.
Definition C.2.5. We define the Zariski topology on an affine scheme Spec R to be the topology
whose closed sets are exactly the sets of the form V pSq for some S Ă R.
We have a scheme-theoretic version of the Nullstellensatz,

Proof. See [Eis95], Theorem I. 1.6.
Remark 45. A particular property of schemes is that it allows points that are not necessarily closed,
meaning that their closure is a bigger subscheme containing that point. We call non-closed point P
in a scheme X a generic point if its closure is the entire scheme.
We now cite a few topological properties of affine schemes :
Definition C.2.7. A topological space X is called reducible if it can be written as X “ X1 Y X2,
for closed subsets X1, X2 Ĺ X. Otherwise it is called irreducible.
Proposition C.2.8. An affine variety Spec R is irreducible if and only if R is an integral domain.
Proposition C.2.9. An affine scheme X is said to be integral if and only if it is irreducible and
reduced.
Definition C.2.10. An affine scheme X is called disconnected if it can be written as X “ X1YX2
for closed subsets X1, X2 Ĺ X with X1 X X2 “ H. Otherwise it is called connected
Definition C.2.11. A topological space is called Noetherian if there is no infinite strictly decreas-
ing chain of closed subsets.
Thus an affine scheme Spec R is Noetherian if and only if R is a Noetherian ring.
Definition C.2.12. The dimension dim X P N Y t8u of a non-empty topological space X is the
supremum of the length of chains of strictly increasing irreducible closed subsets of X.
Proposition C.2.13. For a ring R and an element f P R, we call Dpfq :“ Spec RzV pfq the
distinguished open subset of f in Spec R.
Remark 46. The distinguish open sets form a basis of the Zariski topology of an affine scheme
Spec R in the sense that every open subset U Ă Spec R is an arbitrary union of distinguished opens.
We now define the notion of morphism of schemes. Since schemes usually carry more structure than
typical topological spaces, it is natural that morphism of schemes need to satisfy more conditions
than just being continuous. It does not just have to move points around, it needs to map sections
of structure sheaves in a meaningful way. This is defined below.
Definition C.2.14. A locally ringed space is a ringed space pX, OXq such that each stalk OX,P
for P P X is a local ring.
A morphism of locally ringed spaces from pX, OXq to pY, OY q is
given by a continuous map f : X Ñ Y and for every open subset U Ă Y a ring homomorphism
f ˚
U : OY pUq Ñ OXpf ´1pUqq called the pull-back on U, such that the two following conditions hold :
aq The pull-back maps must be compatible with restriction (this implies there are ring homomor-
phism f ˚
P : OY,fpP q Ñ OX,P on the stalks for every P P X).
bq The ring homomorphism induced by f˚ between the stalks of Y and X must be a local
homomorphism, i.e. for every P P X, we have pf˚
P q´1pIP q “ IfpP q where IP and IfpP q denote
maximal ideals of OX,P and OY,fpP q respectively.
Any affine scheme is a locally ringed space with its structure sheaf (see Proposition II. 2.3. in
[Har83]). Let X be an affine scheme and OX its structure sheaf. For any open subset U Ă X, then
OXpUq is the ring of regular functions on U. This is not to be confused with the ring of rational
functions on U.
92


## Page 94


Definition C.2.15. A scheme is a locally ringed space that has an open cover by affine schemes.
Morphisms of schemes are just morphisms as locally ringed spaces. This makes a category of schemes
denoted Sch.
In the literature, there is often a distinction between separated and non-separated scheme (the
notion corresponds to that of Hausdroff spaces in topology). For sake of simplicity, all the schemes
we consider are assumed to be separated.
Remark 47. All the above topological concepts defined for affine schemes immediately apply to
schemes.
We can now define open and closed subschemes :
Construction C.2.16. Let X be a scheme. We say that Y is a open subscheme of Y whose
underlying space is the subspace Y of X together with an isomorphism of structure sheaf OY with
the restriction OX|Y . Similarly, an open immersion is a morphism X Ñ Y which induces an
isomorphism of X with an open subscheme of Y .
On the other hand, Z is a closed subscheme of X if there is a morphism of locally ringed spaces
i : Z Ñ X that is a closed immersion. This means it has to satisfy the following criteria :
¨ the map i is a homeomorphism of Z onto its image,
¨ the associated sheaf map OX Ñ i˚OZ is surjective with kernel I,
¨ the kernel I is locally generated by sections as an OX-module.
This is further explained in the next subsection.
Even though they are an important class of schemes, we give a quick definition of projective schemes.
Construction C.2.17. Let R be a commutative graded ring, where R “ ‘dě0Rd is its gradation.
The elements of Rdzt0u are said to be homogeneous of degree d. An ideal in a graded ring is called
homogeneous if it is generated by homogeneous elements. The ringed space pSpec R, OSpec Rq
is an affine projective scheme if R is a graded ring as above and Spec R :“ tP Ĳ R |
with P a homogeneous prime idealsu, with the usual structure sheaf. Similarly as above, one define
a projective scheme pX, OXq.
Definition C.2.18. Let X be a scheme. The ring of rational functions on X is the ring RpXq
whose elements are rational functions of X.
Remark 48. We can also define the residue field κpPq for P a point of a locally ringed space
pX, OXq. It is defined as follows κpPq :“ OX,P {mP where mP is the unique maximal ideal of OX,P .
Lemma C.2.19. Let X be an integral scheme, then the function field kpXq “ OX,η “ κpηq for η
the unique generic point of X (see Lemma 2.1.4).
Proof. See [Aut](Tag 01RV).
Definition C.2.20. Let X be an integral scheme. The function field or field of rational func-
tions of X is the field RpXq, that we now denote kpXq.
One powerful aspects of scheme theory is that it allows us to consider varieties/schemes over par-
ticular fields. For example, we talk about a scheme X over a field k, when we consider the map
X Ñ Spec k. Over certain fields, schemes may have different properties which are called relative
properties, since they are now relative to a base scheme. This was the case for most concepts pre-
sented in Section 2.2. In that sense relative properties of schemes can be thought of as properties of
morphisms of scheme.
Definition C.2.21. A morphism of schemes f : X Ñ Y is said to be quasi-compact if Y can
be covered by open affine subschemes pViqiPI such that the pre-images f ´1pViq are compact (in the
usual topological sense) for all i P I
Definition C.2.22. A morphisms f : X Ñ Y of schemes is called proper if it is (separated) of
finite type and universally closed.

An interesting use of the base change is the ability to consider geometric notions. Usually, we want
to work over algebraically closed fields, but not all properties of schemes are invariant under base
change. So we can “force” a scheme to have a certain property over an algebraically closed field
by considering it having that property over its closure. As an illustration we give the following
definition
Definition C.2.24. A scheme X over a field k is said to be geometrically integral if for all field
extension L|k, X ˆk Spec L is integral, then of course X ˆk Spec k is also integral.
There are many ways to define the fibre of a morphism in algebraic geometry
Definition C.2.25. Let f : X Ñ Y be a morphism of schemes, let y P Y and κpyq the residue field
of y with the inclusion morphism being ι : Spec κpyq Ñ Y . We define the fibre of f at y to be the
scheme Xy :“ X ˆY Spec κpyq
C.3
Quasi-coherent Sheaves
So far we have introduced schemes and morphisms between them without mentioning any sheaves
other than the structure sheaves, our review of scheme theory would not be complete without
mentioning sheaves of modules.
Definition C.3.1. Let X be a scheme and OX its structure sheaf. A sheaf of modules on X,
also called a sheaf of OX-modules is a sheaf F on X such that FpUq is an OXpUq-module for all
open subsets U Ă X and all restriction maps are OX-modules homomorphisms in the sense that
pφ ` ψq|U “ φ|U ` ψ|U and pλφq|U “ λ|U ¨ φ|U
for all open subsets U Ă V of X, λ P OXpV q and φ, ψ P FpV q.

aq Let X be a scheme, then OX is a sheaf of modules on X.

set pKer fqpUq :“ kerpfU : FpUq Ñ GpUqq. Ker f is a sheaf of modules on X, we call it the
kernel sheaf of f.
cq For a morphism f : F Ñ G of sheaves on a scheme X and any open subset U Ă X, we set
pIm1 fqpUq :“ impfU : FpUq Ñ GpUqq. Im1 f defines a presheaf of modules but not yet a sheaf.
We define its sheafification Im f the image sheaf of f.

Definition C.3.7. A sheaf of modules F on a scheme X is called quasi-coherent if there is an

isomorphic to the sheaf associated to Mi and Ri-module. Moreover, if Mi is a finitely generated
Ri-module for all i, we say that F is a coherent sheaf

its objects admit an injective resolution which basically mean that the above global section functor
is left exact. This follows from the following result

has enough injectives.

in terms of coherent sheaves
Corollary C.3.15 (Coherent Nakayama). If the stalk of a coherent sheaf is zero at a point P, then
the sheaf restricts to zero in an open neighbourhood of P.

We have seen above that the functor of global sections (which can be thought of as a derived functor

0 Ñ F1pXq Ñ F2pXq Ñ F3pXq Ñ . . .
Sheaf cohomology is basically the study of when the above sequence fails to be exact.

C.5
Analytification
A smooth scheme over C can be seen as a complex manifold. Since we can apply techniques of
complex analysis and differential geometry to complex manifolds, provided we have a nice dictionary
between the language of abstract algebraic geometry and complex manifolds, we could translate those
analytical results into results about schemes.
We first need to define what we mean by an analytic space (complex in our case)
Definition C.5.1. Let U Ă Cn be the Cartesian product of open discs : t|zi| ă 1 | i “ 1, ..., nu,
let f1, ..., fm be holomorphic functions on U and let Y Ă U be the closed subset (for the usual
Euclidean topology) consisting of the zero locus V pf1, ..., fmq, with its structure sheaf the restriction
OY “ OU{pf1, ..., fmq (for OU the sheaf of holomorphic functions on U).
A complex analytic space is a locally ringed space pX, OXq which can be covered by affine open
sets, each of which biholomorphic (as locally ringed spaces) to a space Y as above. (Notice that Y
is open in X but closed in U Ă Cn)
Notice that the sheaf structure is actually a structure of C-modules.
Remark 50. Theses spaces are called analytic because the topology on them is the topology induced
from the usual one on Cn open sets are small, and compatible with analysis (limits, continuity,
holomorphic functions, ...)
Similarly as in the algebraic case, complex analytic spaces are a generalisation of complex manifolds,
indeed, they also allow singularities. For example, let X “ tpu, vq P C2 | uv “ 0u be a complex
analytic space, then it is not a complex manifold since there is no neighbourhood at the origin that
is biholomorphic to an open subset of C.
Definition C.5.2. If X and Y are complex analytical spaces, a morphism φ : X Ñ Y of locally
C-ringed spaces is called a holomorphic map.
As the name of this section suggests, the goal is to associate to an algebraic scheme X over C such
a complex analytic space that we will denote by Xan,
Construction C.5.3. Let X be a scheme of finite type over C. Consider a covering of X by affine
open subsets Yi “ Spec Ai, recall that for all i, Ai is of finite type, so Ai – Crx1, ..., xns{pf1, ..., fmq
with f1, ..., fm polynomials in x1, ..., xn. We can regard those polynomials as holomorphic functions
on Cn and their zero locus form a complex analytic closed subspace Y an
i
in Cn. Since the scheme
X is obtained by glueing the open sets Yi, we can use the same glueing data to glue the analytic
spaces Y an
i
into an analytic space Xan. This is the associated complex analytic space of X.
Remark 51. Notice that the above construction is functorial thus we obtain a functor
an from
the category of finite schemes over C to the category of complex analytical spaces. There is also
a continuous map φ : Xan Ñ X of the underlying topological spaces, which sends Xan bijectively
onto closed points of X.
In his groundbreaking paper [Ser56], Serre proved a plethora of results about the relationship between
a scheme X ans its associated analytic space Xan, we enumerate them below:
Proposition C.5.4. Let X be a scheme over C, locally of finite type and Xan the associated ana-
lytical space. Then the scheme X is
aq non-empty,
bq discrete,
98


## Page 100


cq regular,
dq normal,
eq reduced, or
fq of dimension n
if and only if the associated Xan is as well.
Proof. See [Gro03], Proposition XII. 2.1.
Proposition C.5.5. Let X and Y be two schemes over C locally of finite type, f : X Ñ Y a
morphism of finite type and f an : Xan Ñ Y an the associated analytical morphism induced by f.
Then f is
aq surjective,
bq a closed immersion,
cq an immersion,
dq proper, or
eq finite
if and only if f an is as well.
Proof. See [Gro03], Proposition XII. 3.2.
We say that a complex analytical space pX, OXq is actually a Riemann surface if and only if it is
1-dimensional and smooth (or non-singular). Thus, notice that the above notions also make sense
for Riemann surfaces when considering their structure sheaf of holomorphic functions.
Remark 52. It is interesting to note that in [Ser56], for X a projective scheme over C, Serre also
showed that the analytification functor yields an equivalence of categories between coherent sheaves
on X and coherent analytic sheaves on Xan.
